19	## What to do for each item on your list
20	1. Find the question and the row(s) the item describes. Read the stimulus, the stem, all five choices, the key, and the full explanation (all styles).
21	2. Confirm the defect is real. The list was written by earlier readers and may be wrong or already fixed. If it is not real, record a `skip` with the reason.
22	3. Write the smallest edit that makes the text correct:
23	   - Keep the author's voice, structure and wording everywhere outside the defect.
24	   - A misquote, slip or wrong-letter reference: correct the words.
25	   - Garbled or flattened transcription (scrambled diagrams, run-together words, truncation): reconstruct the intended text from the stimulus and the surrounding explanation. Put each diagram chain on its own line, with `→` arrows. If you cannot reconstruct it with confidence, rewrite only that sentence or chain plainly and correctly.
26	   - A note that never says why its choice is wrong or right (verdict-only, pasted from another letter, identical to another note, empty, placeholder): write a real note of 1–3 sentences that states exactly why, tied to the choice's own words and the stimulus.
27	   - Overbroad rules stated as fact ("this answer is always wrong"), mocking or political asides, Logic Games analogies, claims about answer placement: remove or soften them so the teaching point stands. There are no Logic Games on the current LSAT; never mention them.
28	   - Never change which choice is credited. If you believe the key itself is wrong, record a `flag`, not an edit.
29	4. New or rewritten text rules: no em dashes (use a comma, colon, or a new sentence); no gendered pronouns for people in the stimulus (use the name, "the author", "they"); plain, direct sentences; teach the reasoning, do not just assert it; no filler ("Great question", "it's important to note").
30	5. Double-check every proposal: re-read the edited text next to the choice text and stimulus, and confirm the logic and every quote are exact.

8	# Choice Note problems confirmed by reading
9	
10	Each row was checked against the stimulus and the answer choices in the question bank (`lsatu-wt-parser/public/data`), since the DB rows do not store the choices. Companion to [[revamp-list]].
11	
12	**Key conflicts:** none found. The scan flagged 16 notes that seemed to call a wrong letter correct; every one was a "Yes, the author assumes this, but..." opening. No explanation argues for a letter other than `correct_choice`. Two items point students the wrong way without contradicting the key: PT158-S4-Q22 (the breakdown predicts wrong choice D) and PT108-S3-Q6 (the notes belong to a different question; see side-issues).
13	
14	**Popular traps:** the DB has no answer-choice percentages, and the local markdown that does was out of scope, so "verdict-only note on the popular trap" could not be tested directly. What can be said: 577 explanations have at least one wrong-answer note under 15 words or a bare "not relevant" / "out of scope" verdict (`verdict_only` column in `revamp-inventory.csv` (in this folder)), and in the 100 Weak items that is the typical note.
15

The citizen is arguing for a specific solution to counter the effects of the recession, namely by proposing a tax cut. The premise that connects this solution to the desired effect (job creation) is that if upper-income citizens receive tax cuts, they will invest the extra money, thus creating new jobs.

For this argument to hold, the citizen is assuming that tax cuts for upper-income citizens will directly lead to increased investment. Put another way, the citizen assumes that the money saved from taxes will be used in a way that stimulates economic activity.

A potential objection could be that upper-income citizens might choose to save the extra money or spend it on non-investment ventures, which wouldn't create jobs. Therefore, for the citizen's argument to stand, there must be an assumed link between the tax cuts and increased investment.

The correct answer should indicate that the money gained from a tax cut by upper-income citizens will indeed increase investment, which aligns with choice (D).

-- note E [1eddc2f4-ab1a-4c05-b1c4-53e0791b6b74]

=== STYLE single 7aca97bc-3629-4277-bbd4-5597bdda2c4c
-- step 0 [020f306b-a3e9-4046-8b8f-8e2726f19554] label='Given that ...'
The power plant opened and started discharging wastewater into the river.
-- step 1 [78fa0921-1787-4fe5-870a-f74547986e47] label='Why is it that ...'
Shortly after, anglers downstream caught fewer bass overall?
-- step 2 [30050c0b-3c74-407f-a7ab-42bd91a5178d] label='Evaluate'
This isn't a true paradox. Nothing here is surprising. The stem just wants explanations for a drop in the number of bass caught.

Think about what "number of bass caught" depends on. It takes two things: bass in the water and people fishing for them. So there are two roads to fewer bass caught:
1. **Fewer bass downstream.** They died, got eaten, moved away, or lost their habitat.
2. **Less fishing downstream.** Fewer anglers, or anglers fishing less.

Usually, on EXCEPT questions, three of the four answers we eliminate come at the stimulus from one angle, and the fourth takes a different tactic. Here, expect three answers on road 1 and one sneaking in on road 2.

Most students only think of road 1, because the wastewater feels like it must be hurting the fish. But road 2 is just as good. If half the anglers stop showing up, half as many bass get caught, even if the bass are thriving.
-- step 3 [3a915989-8077-4179-8156-11839c6a5952] label='Goal'
Eliminate four answers that explain fewer bass caught, by either road. The correct answer will do neither, or it will push the other way: more bass or more fishing, which should mean *more* bass caught.

Be careful with any answer about anglers. It isn't out of scope. Anglers are half the equation.
-- note A [bc1e9cd3-871c-463a-ae9e-14108e66a53e]
There are fewer bass to be caught, because an increase in predators has depleted the bass population.
-- note B [09715b96-d8dc-43ac-9da7-8df2738d6c9d]
There are fewer bass to be caught, because the bass have swum upstream or far downstream to cooler water.
-- note C [a7363b92-0e04-4d9f-92f5-559a26c2300a]
If there are more people fishing, we'd expect MORE bass to be caught. This doesn't explain why FEWER bass are being caught.
-- note D [96597a73-05b1-4189-b7a8-85bb2b514718]
There are fewer bass being caught, because there are fewer anglers fishing downstream of the plant.
-- note E [6b186f39-db7c-4cdb-811b-9d07def23aa5]
There are fewer bass to be caught, because destruction of the habitat downstream from the plant has led to a smaller population of bass in the area.

 most similar to the flawed reasoning exhibited in the

=== STYLE standard 8ae7f371-cd8d-4abb-86d4-7c6d0cb19166
-- step 0 [891136a1-2fc9-4375-97d4-85cfbcb65b49] label='Conclusion (So)'
Devan is my enemy.
-- step 1 [3b09f457-6f4e-41a2-a1ad-30dcd8e3b300] label='Evidence (since)'
He does not meet any of these basic criteria for friendship (being kind / offering to help / being compassionate).
-- step 2 [d26f253b-a72f-40df-8a3f-8c40a85aad70] label='Evaluate'
How would we object to this conclusion. Given that Devan has never been kind, offered help, or showed compassion, how could we argue that he is not our enemy? Isn't there some middle ground between "not being a friend" and "being an enemy"? Those aren't binary options. It feels like the author is imposing some False Choice that if someone isn't a friend, they must be an enemy. Maybe Devan is just a stranger or just a neutral party, who isn't necessarily for us but isn't against us.
-- step 3 [09a4986f-7a40-41a5-a887-eecf63025b43] label='Goal'
Let's look for an argument whose conclusion feels like it's swinging to the opposite pole from what the evidence is talking about. i.e. Jim doesn't meet any of the criteria for "best employee". Thus, he is the worst employee.
-- note A [ddedb850-2b46-46e6-8f9e-c03f4c054790]
This argument has a different logical structure from the stimulus. It establishes requirements for being a club officer (two years' standing OR committee member) and concludes that someone who meets neither requirement is not an officer. This is actually valid reasoning � if the requirements are genuinely necessary conditions, failing all of them does disqualify someone. The flaw in the stimulus was jumping from "fails friendship criteria" to "is an enemy" � a false dichotomy between two extreme categories. This answer concludes only that someone is not an officer, which is the direct logical consequence of failing the criteria. There is no leap to an extreme opposite category. It concludes "not-X" from failing the criteria for X, whereas the stimulus concluded "anti-X" from failing the criteria for X.
-- note B [5fea0e4e-46fc-44e6-9853-9e604ee3b872]
This argument is logically valid, not flawed. It states that a plant needs sunny location AND regular watering to thrive. The plant is not in a sunny spot, so at least one necessary condition is unmet. The conclusion � that the plant will not thrive � follows logically. This is straightforward modus tollens: if both A and B are necessary for C, and A is absent, then C will not occur. The stimulus's flaw was a false dichotomy � leaping from "not friend" to "enemy." This answer contains no such leap; it simply concludes that a necessary condition is unmet, therefore the outcome will not occur. There is no jump to an opposite extreme category.
-- note C [655f6da9-c8e4-4ea5-acfc-a2535aca3ade]
This answer perfectly mirrors the stimulus's flawed reasoning. The book has been widely reviewed and received no hostile reviews, so it does not meet the criteria for a "bad" book. The conclusion then leaps to: therefore it must be a masterpiece. This is the same false dichotomy as the stimulus. Just as the stimulus jumped from "not a friend" to "enemy" (ignoring mediocre, average, or indifferent relationships), this answer jumps from "not bad" (no hostile reviews) to "masterpiece" (extreme positive), ignoring the vast middle ground of books that are merely decent, competent, forgettable, or mediocre. The structure is identical: failing to meet the criteria for one extreme category, therefore belonging to the opposite extreme category, with all intermediate categories completely overlooked. A book with no hostile reviews could simply be unremarkable � just as someone who is not a friend could simply be a stranger.
-- note D [a50fc229-f56d-4335-9b73-0ad6407ce44e]
This argument says a decision to develop the northern border logically implies it is not in the town's best interest to use the land for recreation. This is a different type of flaw � it assumes that choosing one use for land automatically means an alternative use is not in the town's interest. The flaw here is about conflating a decision with a judgment about the best interest, or about opportunity cost and land use exclusivity. The stimulus's flaw is a false dichotomy between two extreme relational categories (friend/enemy). This answer does not leap from "not-X" to "opposite of X" � it draws an inference about competing uses of a resource. The logical structures are different: the stimulus treats two categories as exhaustive, while this answer treats two land uses as mutually exclusive and draws an evaluative conclusion about one from the selection of the other.
-- note E [fe5473dc-f56b-44cb-a977-0bdfbfad9bae]
This argument claims that if everyone belonged to certain intellectual professions, society would collapse. This is a conditional claim about a hypothetical universal scenario. The structure is entirely different from the stimulus. The stimulus identifies specific criteria not met by an individual and then classifies that individual in an extreme opposite category. This answer makes a prediction about a hypothetical world where everyone has the same profession. There is no "not-X therefore anti-X" structure, no false dichotomy between two extreme categories, and no ignored middle ground. The flaw, if any, is about the unrealistic hypothetical or about overlooking that society can have some authors without everyone being one. The logical pattern does not match.

This answer just concludes "not qualified for A, so not A." No absurd leap. No false dichotomy. Just basic logic working as intended.
-- note B [bb281af7-f37f-46ab-a0a2-5873045820aa]

Cannot match a flaw that does not exist.

Both arguments looked at one end of the spectrum, found no evidence for it, and catapulted themselves to the opposite extreme. A book with no hostile reviews might just be aggressively mediocre � so bland that nobody bothered to hate it. A person who is not your friend might just be the barista who makes your coffee. Both arguments skipped the enormous middle ground between "bad" and "best" (or "friend" and "enemy") like it did not exist. Perfect parallel.
-- note D [a9b79f92-5188-48ec-81bd-dfb71aef277f]

The first is about confusing a practical decision with a value judgment � you can think parks are great and still choose to build houses. The second is about leaping from one extreme to the opposite. Different logical errors, different aisles in the flaw supermarket.
-- note E [36f906d2-8234-4a92-bcfa-fcec378c662d]
"If everyone were a poet, society would collapse!" This is a completely different kind of argument from "Devan is not nice, so he is my enemy." One is a dramatic hypothetical about what would happen if the whole world changed. The other is a snap judgment about one person based on a false dichotomy. They are not even in the same logical zip code. It is like trying to match a speeding ticket with a parking violation because they both involve cars.

=== STYLE standard 69961312-7b47-4f47-9b92-e935c3f6d94c
-- step 0 [3eb5a31c-e7b1-4892-82e1-79b3a2298844] label='Conclusion (Thus)'
Schools should not require students to take courses in individual academic disciplines but should instead require them to take interdisciplinary courses.
-- step 1 [6ca6b2fd-5897-4c28-855f-80c3def3058d] label='Evidence'
Most problems in daily life can't be solved most effectively, if at all, by applying knowledge from any single academic discipline in isolation.

QID PT158-S3-Q9 | type Weaken | key A
BANK STIM: Consumer advocate: Even relatively minor drug-related
 interactions can still be harmful to patients.
 For example, aspirin taken with fruit juice
 is ineffective. People unaware of this suffer
 unnecessary discomfort or take more aspirin than
 necessary. The government should, therefore,
 require drug companies to notify consumers of all
 known drug-related interactions.
BANK STEM: Which one of the following, if true, most weakens the
 consumer advocate’s argument?
  A: Providing information on minor drug-related interactions would detract from a patient’s attention to serious interactions.
  B: Many drugs have fewer documented drug-related interactions than does aspirin.
  C: Providing information about all drug-related interactions would result in only negligible price increases for consumers.
  D: Current research is such that many drug-related interactions have not yet been identified.
  E: Pharmacists usually draw patients’ attention to printed warnings that are provided with drugs.
BANK KEY: A
TAGS: {'A': 'Correct', 'B': 'Too Weak', 'C': 'Too Weak', 'D': 'No Impact', 'E': 'Strengthens, If Anything'}

=== STYLE standard a7039aed-20e3-447c-9b3d-3671418db577
-- note A [e121ea5c-6f98-454c-b02d-eb3f65891491]
This answer identifies the self-defeating consequence of the proposed policy. The consumer advocate wants all drug interactions disclosed so patients can avoid harmful interactions. But if the volume of information about minor interactions overwhelms patients, they will pay less attention to the serious, potentially life-threatening interactions that matter most. The policy would achieve the opposite of its intended purpose: instead of making patients safer, it would make them less safe by diluting their attention across a massive list of interactions, most of which are minor. The aspirin-and-fruit-juice type interactions would crowd out warnings about interactions that could cause organ damage, severe allergic reactions, or death. This is a classic "more is not always better" weakener — the proposed remedy creates a new problem that is worse than the original one.
-- note B [dda1fcc5-ed53-403d-9cdf-fb01fe50241d]
The fact that many drugs have fewer interactions than aspirin does not undermine the consumer advocate's argument. The advocate's proposal covers all drugs, not just aspirin. Even if most drugs have fewer interactions, the policy still requires disclosure of all known interactions for every drug. A drug with five interactions instead of fifty still requires those five to be disclosed under the proposed policy. The volume of information per drug is not the issue — the advocate's argument is about the principle of comprehensive disclosure regardless of quantity. This answer provides context about the aspirin example but does not challenge the reasoning or the policy recommendation in any meaningful way.
-- note C [bdd9d97b-a74e-4994-a38e-d924c938cc46]
If providing information about all interactions would result in only negligible additional costs to drug companies, this removes a potential practical objection to the policy, but it does not address the argument's logical vulnerability. The consumer advocate's argument could be weakened by showing the policy would be ineffective or counterproductive, not by showing it would be inexpensive. The cost of implementation is a practical concern, and showing that costs are negligible actually makes the policy look more feasible, not less desirable. This answer addresses a potential objection (cost) that the argument never raised, and it addresses it in a way that supports the policy rather than undermining it. The real weakness is about information overload, not financial burden.
-- note D [5f88031f-93d6-4d6e-94cf-1df926d3304b]
The fact that many interactions have not yet been identified is irrelevant to whether the government should require disclosure of those that have been identified. The advocate's proposal covers "all known" interactions, and the existence of unknown ones does not affect the case for disclosing known ones. If anything, this answer might slightly strengthen the advocate's position by suggesting the problem is even larger than currently understood — but that would only affect the scope of future disclosure, not the current proposal. The argument is about whether known interactions should be disclosed, not about the completeness of current research.
-- note E [51687569-98e5-4477-809f-82ca97513ddf]
If pharmacists draw patients' attention to printed warnings, this supports the idea that disclosure actually works — people do read and act on the information they receive. The consumer advocate argues that patients should be given interaction information, and this answer confirms that when warnings are provided, healthcare professionals help ensure patients notice them. Rather than weakening the argument, this answer provides evidence that the disclosure mechanism is effective: warnings are not ignored but are actively communicated by pharmacists. This makes the case for comprehensive disclosure stronger, since it suggests the information would actually reach and influence patients.

=== STYLE brief 23846cf9-b8ba-4bbf-ae6e-1e5014983987
-- note A [e3d87933-356d-497e-9b6c-c203960f83fa]
Information overload from minor interactions would cause patients to overlook serious ones. The policy would backfire: instead of improving safety, it would reduce attention to the interactions that matter most.
-- note B [3e990f9b-66f2-4406-8c9f-bcf7507064bb]
Fewer interactions per drug does not undermine the case for disclosure. The advocate's argument applies to all drugs and all interactions regardless of how many each drug has.
-- note C [b13041e1-55e8-425e-a516-1c7993e4a5ab]
Low costs make the policy more feasible, not less desirable. The argument's weakness is about whether the policy would be effective, not whether it would be expensive. This answer supports the proposal rather than undermining it.
-- note D [a0fea328-aab4-46bf-baf3-e6fb4bee7349]
Unknown interactions do not affect the case for disclosing known ones. The advocate proposes disclosing "all known" interactions; the existence of undiscovered ones is beside the point.
-- note E [f9b3ff50-73bc-44b1-add1-6fdbdc804f33]
Pharmacists helping patients notice warnings confirms that the disclosure system works. This supports rather than undermines the case for requiring more comprehensive warnings.

=== STYLE another_way c9e4bb66-9556-4c1d-810b-7166e413b5c4
-- note A [2b430849-0d80-4b92-aa2d-3e4f0504e14b]
The classic "boy who cried wolf" problem, applied to drug labels. List every single interaction — including "this pill is slightly less effective if you eat a grapefruit" — and patients will stop reading before they get to "this pill combined with that pill will send you to the emergency room." The advocate wanted to help people by giving them more information, but this answer shows that more information would actually make people less safe. When everything is highlighted as important, nothing stands out. The minor interactions would drown out the major ones, and the patients who most need to pay attention would have already tuned out ten warnings ago.
-- note B [c1265eee-e1c0-43a8-ada4-46807d5bd112]
"Most drugs have fewer interactions than aspirin." Okay, so the aspirin example was dramatic. That does not help. The advocate is not arguing "all drugs are as interaction-heavy as aspirin." The advocate is arguing that any unknown interaction can cause harm, so all interactions should be disclosed. Whether that means listing fifty warnings or five per drug does not change the principle. This answer quibbles with the illustration while the main argument stands untouched.
-- note C [f79ca97e-633b-42e0-983a-6ec24a370e07]
"It would not even cost that much!" is an argument FOR the policy, not against it. The question asks what weakens the advocate's position, and cheap implementation makes the proposal more attractive, not less. The real problem is not whether drug companies can afford to print more warnings — it is whether patients will actually benefit from reading them. A free policy that makes patients less safe is still a bad policy. This answer is cheering for the wrong team.
-- note D [b2a50f68-019a-4489-be42-34337f951a17]
"There are interactions we have not found yet." Okay, so the iceberg is bigger than we thought. But the advocate is talking about disclosing the part we can see — the "known" interactions. Undiscovered interactions are a problem for researchers, not for this particular policy debate. It is like telling someone who wants to ban a list of toxic chemicals that there are probably more toxic chemicals out there. That does not make banning the known ones a worse idea; it just means there is more work to do later.
-- note E [81897abc-0e4c-43b4-84bf-a69949d7fbb1]
Pharmacists are actually reading the warnings to patients? That is great news — for the consumer advocate. It means the disclosure system is working: print the warnings, pharmacists make sure patients hear about them, patients make better decisions. This is an argument FOR more disclosure, not against it. If pharmacists are already effective at communicating existing warnings, they would presumably do the same with new ones. The advocate could cite this answer as evidence that the proposed policy would actually work as intended.

QID PT158-S3-Q13 | type Necessary Assumption | key D
BANK STIM: Psychotherapist: The troubles from which a patient
 seeks relief through psychotherapy do not have
 purely internal causes; rather, those troubles
 result in part from the patient’s relationships with
 other people. Hence, to help the patient heal,
 the psychotherapist must focus on the need for
 positive change in those relationships.
BANK STEM: Which one of the following is an assumption the
 psychotherapist’s argument requires?
  A: Psychotherapists cannot help change their patients’ relationships unless those patients focus on other people’s troubles.
  B: At least some psychotherapy patients cannot be healed if a psychotherapist helps them change their relationships with other people.
  C: Those psychotherapy patients who change their relationships with other people will thereby find relief from at least some of their troubles.
  D: No psychotherapist can help a patient heal solely by addressing the internal causes of the patient’s troubles.
  E: If a psychotherapist helps patients focus on troubles that are purely internal, then that psychotherapist thereby provides them the relief they seek.
BANK KEY: D
TAGS: {'A': 'Out of Scope / Too Strong', 'B': 'Out of Scope', 'C': 'Too Strong', 'D': 'Correct', 'E': 'Unrelated to Conclusion'}

=== STYLE standard fa822d0d-41d9-4020-b2b7-b73b0fe61fd6
-- note A [7d84100c-80c4-43e1-bc44-ff2c8ec3860b]
This answer makes two problematic claims. First, it introduces a requirement about patients focusing on "the troubles of other people," which is not what the argument discusses. The argument is about patients' own relationships, not about focusing on other people's troubles. Second, the claim that psychotherapists "cannot help change" patients' relationships without this focus goes far beyond what the argument needs to assume. The argument needs only the assumption that internal-only treatment is insufficient for healing — it does not need any specific claim about what prevents therapists from changing relationships. This answer mischaracterizes the relational focus the argument describes and imposes a requirement the argument does not demand.
-- note B [e4d05c13-b86a-4864-b263-f4266815e4d7]
This answer says some patients cannot be healed even if a therapist helps them change their relationships. The argument's conclusion is that therapists must focus on relational change; the assumption needed is about why that focus is necessary. Knowing that relational change sometimes fails to produce healing does not help establish that relational focus is necessary — if anything, it suggests that relational focus might not be sufficient, which is a different concern entirely. The argument needs the assumption that internal-only treatment is insufficient, not the claim that relational treatment is sometimes insufficient too. This answer addresses the potential limitations of the proposed approach rather than establishing why the approach is necessary in the first place.
-- note C [b67988ae-1e3d-4acb-afad-fbffc64d9a30]
This answer claims that patients who change their relationships will "thereby find relief from their troubles." This is a guarantee — if you change the relationships, healing follows. But the argument does not need this strong a claim. The argument needs only that therapy cannot succeed by addressing internal causes alone — it needs the relational focus to be necessary, not to be sufficient by itself. The argument's conclusion is that therapists must focus on relational change, not that relational change will definitely cure patients. Negate this answer: "some patients who change their relationships will not find relief." The argument survives this negation perfectly well — even if relational change is not guaranteed to work, it could still be a necessary component of therapy. The argument does not promise success; it identifies a necessary focus area.
-- note D [28a1ff5c-7464-4313-8318-d58d89134328]
This answer states that no psychotherapist can help a patient heal solely by addressing internal causes. This is exactly the assumption that bridges the gap between the evidence (troubles are partly relational) and the conclusion (therapy must focus on relationships). If a therapist COULD heal a patient by addressing only internal causes, then the relational focus would not be necessary — it might be helpful but not required. But if internal-only treatment cannot achieve healing, then the relational component becomes essential, and the therapist "must" focus on it. Apply the negation test: "some psychotherapist CAN help a patient heal solely by addressing internal causes." If this were true, the conclusion that therapists must focus on relationships would collapse — internal-only treatment could suffice, making relational focus optional rather than mandatory. The negation destroys the argument, confirming this is a necessary assumption.
-- note E [8db59f5c-2b7f-4f67-a145-5d923bdc865c]
This answer discusses what happens when a therapist focuses on "purely internal" troubles, but the argument's conclusion is about what the therapist must focus on (relationships), not about the consequences of focusing on the wrong thing. Additionally, the claim that focusing on internal troubles "will not help patients address any difficulties they have in their relationships" is a claim about consequences for relationships, while the argument's conclusion is about healing the patient — healing and improving relationships are related but not identical goals. The argument needs the assumption that internal-only treatment cannot achieve healing, not a specific claim about whether internal-focused therapy affects relationships. This answer addresses a tangential consequence rather than the core logical link between evidence and conclusion.

=== STYLE brief 66c03c83-3a81-4671-8c3a-d9a1b10d9d3c
-- note A [0355422e-719c-4e8e-8169-d54faecb96a1]
The argument discusses patients' own relationships, not "the troubles of other people." Additionally, the claim about therapists being unable to change relationships is stronger and different from what the argument assumes.
-- note B [f64d7fb8-84c9-4a7a-b441-2ce6e08e3bd1]
Some patients not being healed despite relational change does not establish why relational focus is necessary. The argument needs the assumption that internal-only treatment is insufficient, not that relational treatment sometimes fails.
-- note C [de0f822a-25d5-4e92-9cfa-3b71a0058718]
This guarantees that relational change produces healing, but the argument only needs relational focus to be necessary, not sufficient. The argument survives the negation of this claim: even if relational change does not always work, it could still be a required focus area.
-- note D [669c3152-0677-4755-b343-a7f6e36d0281]
If internal-only treatment cannot heal patients, then relational focus becomes necessary. Negate it: "a therapist CAN heal by addressing only internal causes." This destroys the conclusion, confirming the assumption is necessary.
-- note E [a9dfcc78-8a7e-45ca-bd78-4b606d5baf92]
This discusses the consequences of internal-only therapy for relationships, but the argument needs an assumption about why relational focus is necessary for healing. The connection between therapy approach and relational outcomes is tangential.

=== STYLE another_way 5edd42f4-900b-47e3-a684-c836f4eda634
-- note A [684a563d-8a36-4b6a-ba3d-6d4047def0d3]
"Focus on the troubles of other people" — wait, what? The argument is about patients working on their OWN relationships, not becoming amateur therapists for everyone else. And the "cannot help change" part is a claim about what is impossible, which is way more extreme than what the argument needs. The argument just needs "internal-only treatment is not enough for healing." This answer is talking about a different thing and saying too much about it.
-- note B [0983a528-632f-413c-b314-6a8426065963]
Some patients cannot be healed even if their relationships improve? That is a bummer for those patients, but it does not answer the question the argument needs answered. The argument needs to know WHY therapy must focus on relationships — and the answer is that internal-only treatment is not enough. Knowing that relationship-focused therapy also sometimes fails is a completely separate issue. It is like asking "why do I need to eat?" and being told "even people who eat sometimes get sick." True, but irrelevant to why eating is necessary.
-- note C [f834bf34-4972-4456-8f57-90173656756d]
This answer promises a guarantee: change your relationships, find relief. But the argument never makes that promise. The argument says therapy MUST focus on relationships — it does not say doing so will definitely work. Flip this answer: "some people change their relationships and still have troubles." Does the argument fall apart? No — the therapist's argument is about what therapy needs to include, not about what therapy can guarantee. Necessity is not the same as a guarantee, and this answer confuses the two.
-- note D [22509500-10be-4197-97c0-44c089c03a6f]
The argument says therapists MUST focus on relationships. That "must" is doing a lot of work — it means there is no other way. But why? Because if a therapist could heal a patient by working only on internal stuff, then the relational focus would not be mandatory. This answer locks that door: nobody can heal a patient through internal-only treatment. If that is true, the therapist has no choice but to address the relationships. Try the negation: "Actually, a therapist CAN heal someone by just working on internal stuff." If that were possible, "must focus on relationships" would become "maybe focus on relationships if you feel like it." The argument crumbles. So this assumption is necessary — it is what makes "must" the right word.
-- note E [f33a7b81-1efd-4bc9-994a-66c3088d8167]
This answer says "focusing on internal stuff will not fix relationship problems." Okay, but that is not quite what the argument needs. The argument is about healing the patient, not specifically about fixing their relationships. These overlap, but they are not the same thing. A patient could heal (feel better, function well) without their relationships being directly improved, or vice versa. The argument needs the assumption that internal-only therapy cannot heal — not the claim that internal-only therapy cannot improve relationships. Close, but it is aiming at the wrong target.

BANK STIM: Situation: A physical therapist wants her patients to
 derive more enjoyment from the challenge of
 developing physical skills. She also wants them
 to spend more time practicing those skills. Analysis: Success in meeting the first objective will bring
 about success in meeting the second.
BANK STEM: The analysis provided for the situation above could be
 appropriate for each of the following situations except:
  A: A math teacher wants her students to understand the mathematical principles taught in her course and wants them to apply these principles routinely in everyday life.
  B: A software manufacturer wants its customers to be more satisfied with its product, and it wants them to place fewer calls to the service representative about how to use the product.
  C: A librarian wants fewer of the books borrowed from the library to be lost or stolen, and he wants more of the library’s books to be returned on time.
  D: A hardware retail company wants to construct a new, larger warehouse and wants its employees to help plan how the old warehouse will be expanded to meet any need for more space.
  E: A concert series director wants to present a more varied repertoire and wants to attract new patrons.
BANK KEY: D
TAGS: {'A': 'Applicable', 'B': 'Applicable', 'C': 'Applicable', 'D': 'Correct', 'E': 'Applicable'}

=== STYLE standard 24de3482-12b3-4e23-8667-4c33dca83d01
-- note A [e25443f2-441f-4c69-80ad-a65ef4872600]
The teacher wants students to (1) understand mathematical principles and (2) enjoy learning math. The analysis pattern applies: if students understand the principles being taught (objective 1), they will naturally enjoy the learning experience more (objective 2). Understanding creates engagement, which creates enjoyment. This mirrors the original: achieving a cognitive/experiential improvement (understanding/enjoyment) leads to a behavioral outcome (enjoyment/more time). The causal link between comprehension and enjoyment is a standard motivational connection — people tend to enjoy activities they understand and can succeed at. This fits the pattern.
-- note B [bc3c31a7-2d2e-4e8f-a53b-154f72002871]
The software manufacturer wants customers to (1) be more satisfied with its product and (2) have customers recommend the product to others. The analysis pattern applies: if customers are more satisfied (objective 1), they will naturally recommend the product to others (objective 2). Satisfied customers become advocates — this is the core logic of word-of-mouth marketing. The causal link is direct and intuitive: positive experience leads to positive recommendation. This perfectly mirrors the original's pattern where an internal state improvement (satisfaction/enjoyment) leads to a desired behavioral outcome (recommendations/more practice time).
-- note C [92be04b0-a400-450b-90a4-779c40630517]
The librarian wants (1) fewer borrowed books to be lost or stolen and (2) borrowers to return books in good condition. The analysis pattern applies: if fewer books are lost or stolen (objective 1), borrowers are treating books more responsibly overall, which means they will also return books in better condition (objective 2). Both objectives are symptoms of the same underlying behavior — responsible handling of borrowed materials. Fostering the care that prevents loss and theft (objective 1) naturally extends to the care that preserves book condition (objective 2). The causal mechanism is about a general improvement in responsibility that manifests in multiple positive outcomes.
-- note D [f63b631f-cc3c-4523-aa28-336b516f0d0b]
The hardware company wants (1) to construct a new, larger warehouse and (2) its employees to start working on Saturdays. These two objectives have no natural causal connection. Building a bigger warehouse does not cause employees to be willing to work Saturdays. Unlike every other answer, where achieving the first objective creates the conditions for the second, these two goals are completely independent. A new warehouse is a physical infrastructure project; Saturday work is a labor scheduling decision. There is no mechanism by which constructing a building leads to employees agreeing to work an extra day. The company would need separate strategies for each goal. This breaks the pattern where "success in meeting the first objective brings about success in meeting the second."
-- note E [e7a81700-f303-47a9-ba20-f4ed5371b17c]
The concert series director wants (1) to present a more varied repertoire and (2) to attract new audience members. The analysis pattern applies: if the concert series offers more varied programming (objective 1), it will naturally attract new audience members who are drawn to the expanded offerings (objective 2). Variety broadens appeal — programming that previously only attracted fans of one genre now attracts fans of multiple genres. The causal link is direct: diversified content attracts a diversified audience. This mirrors the original pattern where an improvement in the offering (variety/enjoyment) leads to a desired participation outcome (new audience/more practice time).

=== STYLE brief acbbd2de-8970-4bc0-beab-0fbd91717bed
-- note A [b92e57c6-35af-4f54-b704-d91981fdf47b]
Understanding math principles (objective 1) naturally leads to enjoying math more (objective 2). Comprehension creates engagement and enjoyment. This fits the "achieving #1 causes #2" pattern.
-- note B [1e702ff3-c085-4cea-b8fb-eef75c6dd706]
Customer satisfaction (objective 1) naturally leads to customers recommending the product (objective 2). Happy customers tell others. This fits the pattern directly.
-- note C [198bbf02-b566-424f-ad4a-f5580707af78]
Reducing book loss and theft (objective 1) reflects more responsible borrowing behavior, which naturally leads to books being returned in better condition (objective 2). Both stem from the same underlying improvement in care.
-- note D [68344260-fe72-4c45-9b93-66d7edf83fc3]
Building a larger warehouse (objective 1) has no causal connection to employees working Saturdays (objective 2). These are independent goals requiring separate efforts. The "achieving #1 causes #2" pattern does not apply here.
-- note E [cfcd95c1-8ad5-4154-be57-a7b99baee27a]
More varied programming (objective 1) naturally attracts new audience members (objective 2). Greater variety broadens appeal, bringing in people who were not interested before. The pattern fits.

=== STYLE another_way 517049b1-0ca3-4df1-b1dc-33d1871a5312
-- note A [c7ac1021-1f5c-4c82-8e85-43c1e99cd4cf]
Help students actually get math, and they will start enjoying it. Classic "competence breeds enjoyment" logic. Nobody has fun failing at long division, but once the light bulb turns on, suddenly math class is not so bad. This fits the pattern perfectly: nail objective one (understanding) and objective two (enjoyment) follows naturally.
-- note B [3594fdaa-c6f9-4d6b-a176-f5ac5952d7f3]
Make customers happy, and they will spread the word. This is Marketing 101 — satisfied customers become walking advertisements. Objective one (satisfaction) leads directly to objective two (recommendations). Same logic as the original: positive internal experience produces positive external behavior. Pattern match confirmed.
-- note C [22716cd8-2c98-4a11-82f4-38bb3795a9f9]
If people stop losing and stealing books, they are probably also going to start treating them better in general. The borrower who carefully tracks their library books is the same borrower who is not going to use one as a coaster. Both objectives are branches of the same tree: responsible book behavior. Achieve one, and the other follows. Pattern fits.
-- note D [082803d5-6e7e-44ae-a80c-d61b6931622d]
Build a new warehouse and suddenly employees want to work Saturdays? In what universe? "Hey everyone, we got a bigger building!" "Wonderful — sign me up for the weekend shift!" These two objectives have absolutely nothing to do with each other. One is a construction project, the other is a scheduling negotiation. Every other answer has a clear "achieve A and B follows" logic. This one has... nothing. It is like saying "I want to paint my house AND I want my neighbor to learn French." Good luck finding the causal connection there. This is the odd one out.
-- note E [4bcff6b7-e643-40c6-a68a-78656833a06f]
Play more kinds of music, attract more kinds of listeners. If the concert series was all Beethoven all the time, it only gets the Beethoven crowd. Throw in some jazz, some contemporary stuff, and suddenly people who would never have shown up before are buying tickets. Variety is the spice of audience building. Objective one (varied repertoire) leads naturally to objective two (new audience members). Pattern fits like a glove.

QID PT112-S1-Q9 | type Paradox | key B
BANK STIM: Raisins are made by drying grapes in the sun. Although some of the sugar in the grapes is caramelized in the process, nothing is added. Moreover, the only thing removed from the grapes is the water that evaporates during the drying, and water contains no calories or nutrients. The fact that raisins contain more iron per calorie than grapes do is thus puzzling.
BANK STEM: Which one of the following, if true, most helps to explain why raisins contain more iron per calorie than do grapes?
  A: Since grapes are bigger than raisins, it takes several bunches of grapes to provide the same amount of iron as a handful of raisins does.
  B: Caramelized sugar cannot be digested, so its calories do not count toward the calorie content of raisins.
  C: The body can absorb iron and other nutrients more quickly from grapes than from raisins because of the relatively high water content of grapes.
  D: Raisins, but not grapes, are available year-round, so many people get a greater share of their yearly iron intake from raisins than from grapes.
  E: Raisins are often eaten in combination with other iron-containing foods, while grapes are usually eaten by themselves.
BANK KEY: B
TAGS: {'A': 'Reinforces Surprise', 'B': 'Correct', 'C': 'Out of Scope: rate of absorption', 'D': 'Reinforces Surprise', 'E': 'Out of Scope: accompanying foods'}

=== STYLE single f1016766-4dda-43be-86f3-38cd9e5a63a1
-- step 0 [64a559ab-3554-449d-b1c6-c8be5a1d3df9] label='Given that'
Raisins are made from grapes, and nothing is added in the process,
-- step 1 [895a4cc8-1063-43ec-b6b3-6bd488ded1ae] label='Why is it that'
Raisins contain more iron per calorie than do grapes?
-- step 2 [8cd33db3-2316-4cf2-a60c-2fb8d9c7532f] label='Evaluate'
There must be something about the process of going from a grape to a raisin that increases the iron per calorie.
A ratio like Iron/Calorie can be increased two ways: increase the numerator (add more iron) or decrease the denominator (subtract calories).
The facts we're provided about the process of going from grape to raisin are these: - some sugar is caramelized - nothing is added - water evaporates (but water contains no calories)
-- step 3 [572ad562-e5ee-48ef-9bb9-e8d5bdb6eeb7] label='Goal'
Since there's no way to add more iron, we want an answer to talk to us about how going from a grape to a raisin sheds calories.
-- note A [6e9c1d75-6ec3-4bc7-998e-81bdbfd978d8]
This reinforces the fact that raisins are more iron-rich than grapes (you'd only need a handful of raisins to get the same iron you'd get out of eating several bunches of grapes).

But it doesn't explain **why** raisins are more iron-rich than grapes.
-- note B [4b8dc742-a7bf-4774-8651-b05626d2aafc]
This is offering us a story of how a grape sheds calories en route to being a raisin.

The grape had 20mg of iron and 10 calories. **2 mg iron per calorie**

The raisin keeps that 20mg of iron. "The only thing removed from the grapes is water".

But the sugar in the grape caramelizes as it becomes a raisin. And when we measure how many calories something has, we don't count calories in caramelized sugar (because the body can't digest it).

So when we measure the raisin that formed from that 10 calorie grape, we get a number lower than 10.

Some of the grape's 10 calories were from sugar. When that sugar caramelized, then those sugar-calories stop being counted.

If there were 3 calories from the sugar, then now that the grape has caramelized into a raisin, that 10 calorie grape will only be a 7 calorie raisin.

And if the raisin has 20mg of iron and is only 7 calories, then it's close to being **3 mg iron per capita**.

So this answer allows us to explain why a raisin made from a given grape can have more iron per calorie than the grape, even though no iron is added and only water is removed.
-- note C [39f8cd3d-4094-4be7-a564-4c7128c4fac8]
We're trying to explain why a raisin has more iron per calorie than the grape it formed from.

The paradox is only about the chemistry of the grape/raisin. It has nothing to do with organisms that consume grapes or raisins. So it's irrelevant how a grape or raisin interacts with a human body.
-- note D [b17fa1d6-2758-436c-835c-e3180199910c]
This reinforces the fact that raisins are more iron-rich than grapes (people get more of their yearly iron from raisins than from grapes).

But it doesn't explain **why** raisins are more iron-rich than grapes.
-- note E [8daac9a6-c4c7-4a9d-82e6-d273fc29c439]
We're trying to explain why a raisin has more iron per calorie than the grape from which it was formed.

The paradox is only about the chemistry of the grape/raisin. It has nothing to do with any other foods you might later mix with raisins or grapes. This answer feels like a correct answer, because it feels like we're offering an alternate explanation for why raisins seem to have more iron than grapes. ("It's not because raisins are inherently more iron-heavy, it's just that they're eaten with other foods that supply iron.")

But we are accepting as a given the fact that raising are more iron-heavy than grapes. We need to explain why they are so.

QID PT127-S3-Q5 | type Main Conclusion | key D
BANK STIM: Gardener: Researchers encourage us to allow certain kinds of weeds to grow among garden vegetables because they can repel caterpillars from the garden. While it is wise to avoid unnecessary use of insecticides, the researchers’ advice is premature. For all we know, those kinds of weeds can deplete the soil of nutrients and moisture that garden crops depend on, and might even attract other kinds of damaging pests.
BANK STEM: Which one of the following most accurately expresses the main conclusion of the gardener’s argument?
  A: To the extent that it is possible to do so, we should eliminate the use of insecticides in gardening.
  B: Allowing certain kinds of weeds to grow in vegetable gardens may contribute to a net increase in unwanted garden pests.
  C: Allowing the right kinds of weeds to grow in vegetable gardens can help toward controlling caterpillars without the use of insecticides.
  D: We should be cautious about the practice of allowing certain kinds of weeds to grow among garden vegetables.
  E: We should be skeptical about the extent to which certain kinds of weeds can reduce the presence of caterpillars in gardens.
BANK KEY: D
TAGS: {'A': 'Evidence', 'B': 'Evidence', 'C': 'Opposing Evidence', 'D': 'Correct', 'E': 'Unsupported'}

QID PT130-S1-Q13 | type Main Conclusion | key B
BANK STIM: It is a given that to be an intriguing person, one must be able to inspire the perpetual curiosity of others. Constantly broadening one's abilities and extending one's intellectual reach will enable one to inspire that curiosity. For such a perpetual expansion of one's mind makes it impossible to be fully comprehended, making one a constant mystery to others.
BANK STEM: Which one of the following most accurately expresses the conclusion drawn in the argument above?
  A: To be an intriguing person, one must be able to inspire the perpetual curiosity of others.
  B: If one constantly broadens one's abilities and extends one's intellectual reach, one will be able to inspire the perpetual curiosity of others.
  C: If one's mind becomes impossible to fully comprehend, one will always be a mystery to others.
  D: To inspire the perpetual curiosity of others, one must constantly broaden one's abilities and extend one's intellectual reach.
  E: If one constantly broadens one's abilities and extends one's intellectual reach, one will always have curiosity.
BANK KEY: B
TAGS: {'A': 'Problem', 'B': 'Correct', 'C': 'Premise', 'D': 'Reversal', 'E': 'Unsupported'}

QID PT130-S1-Q24 | type Weaken | key E
BANK STIM: The Kiffer Forest Preserve, in the northernmost part of the Abbimac Valley, is where most of the bears in the valley reside. During the eight years that the main road through the preserve has been closed the preserve's bear population has nearly doubled. Thus, the valley's bear population will increase if the road is kept closed.
BANK STEM: Which one of the following, if true, most undermines the argument?
  A: Most of the increase in the preserve's bear population over the past eight years is due to migration.
  B: Only some of the increase in the preserve's bear population over the past eight years is due to migration of bears from other parts of the Abbimac Valley.
  C: Only some of the increase in the preserve's bear population over the past eight years is due to migration of bears from outside the Abbimac Valley.
  D: The bear population in areas of the Abbimac Valley outside the Kiffer Forest Preserve has decreased over the past eight years.
  E: The bear population in the Abbimac Valley has remained about the same over the past eight years.
BANK KEY: E
TAGS: {'A': 'Opposite / Unclear Impact', 'B': 'Opposite / Unclear Impact', 'C': 'Opposite / Unclear Impact', 'D': 'Unclear Impact / Too Weak', 'E': 'Correct'}

=== STYLE single 89559164-17df-48cd-9e57-99c267e3f698
-- step 0 [67146e1d-9152-4f1a-86e1-7f598446b47e] label='Conclusion'
If the road through the preserve is kept close, the Valley's bear population will increase.
-- step 1 [fff2ff8a-0059-4549-a91c-76cd03fd1f72] label='Evidence'
During the eight years that the road has been closed, the population of bears inside the Kiffer Preserve has nearly doubled. Most of the bears in the Valley live in this Preserve.
-- step 2 [ba503d7e-5524-4b02-b9a5-3db1733a5a8a] label='Evaluation'
The author is making a causal assumption. She she's that the preserve's population of bears has nearly doubled while the road has been closed and then assumes that "the road being closed is causing there to be more bears there". Maybe, but there could be other ways to explain the uptick in bear population, so we could weaken the argument by coming up with a different way to explain why the bear population has gone up. The author is also shifting from talking about a population increase in the preserve to talking about a population increase in the valley. We might want to think about the difference there. Maybe the population goes up in the preserve (which is in the valley) but goes down in other parts of the valley. Finally, the author is making a prediction. Even if we granted that the road closure was the causal reason for the uptick in bears, there could be factors now that are different from 8 years ago. Maybe something meaningful has changed where further road closures would not have continuing effect on increasing the bear population (maybe they have a max population limited by the size of that habitat).
-- step 3 [6dc64020-dea9-4681-bb33-7dd7272e447f] label='Goal'
There are lots of attack points. Let's focus on the anti-conclusion and look for a way to argue that "we could keep the road closed, but it wouldn't lead to an increase in the valley's bear population."
-- note A [4e9766b9-5281-45af-be66-284fdae9fe8e]
This may seem to weaken the author's causal assumption that the road closure is boosting the bear population, because it seems to provide an alternative explanation: the uptick isn't because of the road closure, it's because of migration. However, is that an alternative explanation or part of the same causal story?
Author could say: having this road closed is creating a tranquil, undisturbed natural preserve, which attracts bears. Look, over the last 8 years, we have tons of new bears in this preserve, and (according to this answer) they didn't come from other parts of the valley, they migrated here from elsewhere! If we keep the road closed, we'll see more of that.
As we'll see in correct answer E, if the new bears coming to the preserve were just coming from elsewhere in the valley, then they wouldn't be raising the total number of bears in the valley. It would be the same valley bear population as before, just re-shuffled into different areas. So being told that the new bears are from outside the valley strengthens the author's case. This answer doesn't clarify whether these bears have migrated from elsewhere in the valley or from outside the valley.
-- note B [405a7e99-b854-4160-89f4-2551e9b4e889]
For all the reasons cited in the explanation for choice A, this also strengthens. By saying "only some new bears came from other parts of the valley", we learn that "some new bears came from outside the valley", which helps the author argue that the valley's bear population has increased and will continue to do so.
-- note C [0fcfbd3e-6b87-4b14-922d-35e8313dad60]
This has an identical impact to B, so neither answer could be correct. They're both using the formulation "only some", which reveals that "some of the increase came from outside the valley, some from inside the valley". The ones coming from outside the valley strengthen the author's case. The ones coming from inside the Valley (if they are outside the Preserve) would weaken the author's case.
-- note D [08fcf633-ee18-4098-a920-956fdcca2316]
This looks tempting: if the population in the preserve is going up, but in the area of the valley that's outside of the preserve it's going down, that looks like bears are traveling from other parts of the valley into the preserve.
That scenario weakens the argument: it explains how the population in the preserve is climbing while allowing for the valley's bear population to have stayed the same overall.
But is this answer actually saying that the bears have gone from the non-preserve parts of the valley to the preserve? No, we're just adding that. This answer ends up being less convincing than the best answer. Because this answer is at least suggestive of an alternate explanation that would hurt the author's argument (population increase in preserve is just from other parts of the valley), this could be a correct answer on a Weaken question. But it's not the best available answer in this case: E is way stronger at delivering the same objection.
-- note E [aba6c5a6-08e2-4571-b8d3-73f10cc10b98]
If the valley's total population has stayed the same for the past eight years during the road closure, that means two things:
1. the increase in bears in the preserve must have been accompanied by a decrease in bears in the non-preserve parts of the valley (maybe they migrated over, or maybe the death rate went up in the non-preserve parts while the birth-rate went up inside the preserve)
2. the author no longer has his correlation to stand on: during those 8 years of road closure, the preserve's population increased. But during those 8 years of road closure, the valley's population did not increase. So why should we believe the conclusion that if we keep the road closed, we'll see a population increase in the valley? If we haven't seen one yet, why would we expect one now?

QID PT131-S3-Q11 | type Strengthen | key B
BANK STIM: Predictions that printed books will soon be replaced by books in electronic formats such as CD-ROM are exaggerated. While research libraries may find an electronic format more convenient for scholars and scientists, bookstores and public libraries will stock books in the format desired by the general public, which will be something other than an electronic format.
BANK STEM: Which one of the following, if true, most strengthens the argument?
  A: Scholars and scientists find an electronic format for books the most convenient one for quick searching and cross-referencing.
  B: Publishers will continue to print books in the format stocked by bookstores and public libraries.
  C: Scholars and scientists do not usually conduct their research in public libraries.
  D: At some bookstores and libraries, the popularity of books on tape and of videos is beginning to rival that of printed books.
  E: Some members of the general public prefer to purchase books in an electronic format rather than borrow them from the library.
BANK KEY: B
TAGS: {'A': 'Supports an Opposing Point', 'B': 'Correct', 'C': 'Supports an Opposing Point', 'D': 'Weaken', 'E': 'Weaken'}

BANK STIM: A recent study of major motion pictures revealed that the vast majority of their plots were simply variations on plots that had been used many times before. Despite this fact, many people enjoy seeing several new movies each year.
BANK STEM: Each of the following, if true, would contribute to an explanation of the apparent discrepancy in the information above except:
  A: Movies based on standard plots are more likely to be financially successful than are ones based on original plots.
  B: If the details of their stories are sufficiently different, two movies with the same basic plot will be perceived by moviegoers as having different plots.
  C: Because of the large number of movies produced each year, the odds of a person seeing two movies with the same general plot structure in a five-year period are fairly low.
  D: A certain aesthetic pleasure is derived from seeing several movies that develop the same plot in slightly different ways.
  E: Although most modern movie plots have been used before, most of those previous uses occurred during the 1940s and 1950s.
BANK KEY: A

BANK STIM: Style manual: Archaic spellings and styles of punctuation in direct quotations from older works are to be preserved if they occur infrequently and do not interfere with a reader's comprehension. However, if they occur frequently, the editor may modernize them, inserting a note with an explanation to this effect in the text, or if similar modernizing has been done in more than one quotation, inserting a general statement in the preface. On the other hand, obvious typographical errors in quotations from modern works may be corrected without explanation.
BANK STEM: Which one of the following follows logically from the statements above?
  A: If an editor corrects the spelling of a quoted word and the word occurs only once in the text, then an explanation should appear in a note or in the text.
  B: An editor may modernize an archaic spelling of a word found in a modern work without providing an explanation.
  C: An editor should modernize an archaic spelling of a word that is quoted from an older work if the spelling interferes with reader comprehension.
  D: An editor may modernize punctuation directly quoted from an older work if that punctuation occurs frequently and interferes with reader comprehension.
  E: If an editor modernizes only one of several similar instances of quoted archaic punctuation, an explanation should appear in the preface of the work.
BANK KEY: D

BANK STIM: Engine noise from boats traveling through killer whales’ habitats ranges in frequency from 100 hertz to 3,000 hertz, an acoustical range that overlaps that in which the whales communicate through screams and squeals. BANK STEM: Which one of the following most logically completes the argument?
  A: younger killer whales are better able to tolerate engine noise from boats than older whales are
  B: killer whales are less likely to attempt to communicate with one another when boat engines are operating nearby
  C: noise from boat engines may impair killer whales’ ability to communicate
  D: killer whales are most likely to prefer areas where boat traffic is present, but light
  E: killer whales would probably be more successful in finding food if boats did not travel through their habitats

QID PT134-S3-Q26 | type Sufficient Assumption | key E
BANK STIM: Acquiring complete detailed information about all the pros and cons of a product one might purchase would clearly be difficult and expensive. It is rational not to acquire such information unless one expects that the benefits of doing so will outweigh the cost and difficulty of doing so. Therefore, consumers who do not bother to acquire such information are thereby behaving rationally.
BANK STEM: The conclusion of the argument is properly drawn if which one of the following is assumed?
  A: Rational consumers who do not expect that the benefits outweigh the cost and difficulty of acquiring detailed information about a product they might purchase usually do not bother to acquire such information.
  B: Whenever it is rational not to acquire detailed information about a product, it would be irrational to bother to acquire such information.
  C: The benefits of acquiring detailed information about a product one might purchase usually do not outweigh the cost and difficulty of doing so.
  D: Rational consumers usually expect that the benefits of acquiring detailed information about a product they might purchase would not outweigh the cost and difficulty of doing so.
  E: Consumers who do not bother to acquire complete detailed information about a product they might purchase do not expect that the benefits of acquiring such information will outweigh the cost and difficulty of doing so.
BANK KEY: E
TAGS: {'A': 'Reversal', 'B': 'Negation', 'C': 'Too Weak', 'D': 'Negation', 'E': 'Correct'}

BANK STIM: Lutsina: Because futuristic science fiction does not need to represent current social realities, its writers can envisage radically new social arrangements. Thus it has the potential to be a richer source of social criticism than is conventional fiction. Priscilla: That futuristic science fiction writers more skillfully envisage radically new technologies than new social arrangements shows how writers’ imaginations are constrained by current realities. Because of this limitation, the most effective social criticism results from faithfully presenting the current social realities for critical examination, as happens in conventional fiction.

QID PT136-S4-Q26 | type Main Conclusion | key B
BANK STIM: Anthropologist: Many people think that if human language evolved, then something like it must be present in those species most closely related to humans, such as chimpanzees. They reason that since new traits evolve gradually, something like human language, albeit cruder, must exist in some species from which humans evolved. This general line of argument may be reasonable, but it simply does not follow that chimpanzees have anything like human language, because humans did not evolve from chimpanzees. While chimpanzees are indeed closely related to humans, this is because both evolved from a common ancestor. The evolution of human language might easily have begun after the extinction of that common ancestor.
BANK STEM: Which one of the following most accurately expresses the main conclusion of the anthropologist's argument?
  A: Humans did not evolve from chimpanzees, but rather from some extinct species.
  B: The assumption that something like human language must exist in some species from which humans evolved has no clearcut linguistic implications for chimpanzees.
  C: The communicative systems of chimpanzees are cruder than human language.
  D: Human language is a by-product of human intelligence, which chimpanzees lack.
  E: The evolution of human language began after the disappearance of an extinct species from which both humans and chimpanzees evolved.
BANK KEY: B
TAGS: {'A': 'Evidence', 'B': 'Correct', 'C': 'Opposing Point', 'D': 'Out of Scope', 'E': 'Evidence / Too Strong'}

QID PT148-S3-Q8 | type Sufficient Assumption | key D
BANK STIM: In the earliest video games, the player typically controlled the movements of a simple icon on the screen. But in newer video games, players often control the movements of detailed human figures—a feature possible because of the greater technical sophistication of these games. It is hard for players to identify with these figures, since the players can see that the figures represent other people. Thus, in this respect the technical sophistication of the newer video games often makes them less compelling to players.
BANK STEM: The conclusion of the argument can be properly drawn if which one of the following is assumed?
  A: There are no newer, more technically sophisticated video games in which the player controls the movements of a simple icon on the screen.
  B: Most early video games in which the player controlled a simple icon on the screen were in other respects less compelling to players than newer video games.
  C: The technical sophistication necessary for creating detailed human figures in video games cannot in itself make those video games fully compelling even to players who identify with those figures.
  D: When players cannot easily identify with the figure or icon whose movements they control in a video game, they usually find that game less compelling than it otherwise would be.
  E: If some aspect of a video game’s technical sophistication makes it less compelling to players, then that video game contains a human figure with whom it is difficult for players to identify.
BANK KEY: D
TAGS: {'A': 'Supports a Premise', 'B': 'Out of Scope', 'C': 'Too Weak', 'D': 'Correct', 'E': 'Reversal'}

QID PT148-S3-Q19 | type Agree/Disagree | key C
BANK STIM: Ana: On libertarian principles, I oppose the proposed smoking ban. It is not the government’s business to prevent people from doing things that harm only themselves.
Pankaj: But keep in mind that the ban would apply only to smoking in public places. People could still smoke all they want in private.
BANK STEM: The dialogue provides the most support for the claim that Ana and Pankaj disagree over whether
  A: it is the government’s business to prevent people from harming themselves
  B: government should be restrained by libertarian principles
  C: the proposed smoking ban is intended to prevent harm only to smokers themselves
  D: the proposed ban would prohibit smoking in public places
  E: there are cases in which government should attempt to regulate private behavior
BANK KEY: C
TAGS: {'A': 'Out of Scope Person 2', 'B': 'Out of Scope Person 2', 'C': 'Correct', 'D': 'Unknown Person 1', 'E': 'Out of Scope Person 2'}

=== STYLE single bec64040-7b47-40b1-b728-b9e4400c8cc1
-- step 0 [1065ffe0-ba4e-4097-bc0b-700539237a92] label="Ana's 1st Claim"
On libertarian, principles, I oppose the proposed smoking ban.
*Does Pankaj say he does not oppose the smoking ban?*

No, nothing definitive. He pushes back against Ana's opposition, but that doesn't necessarily mean that he supports the ban. On LSAT, it's common for someone to disagree with person A's rationale for reaching position X, even though they don't necessarily disagree with position X.
-- step 1 [ca6d1705-3f5c-4664-8d1c-488a1a167d09] label="Ana's 2nd Claim"
It isn't the government's business to prevent people from doing things that harm only themselves.
*Does Pankaj say he thinks that it* **is** *the government's business sometimes to prevent people from doing things that harm only themselves?*

No, he doesn't say anything about the government or what its role is.
-- step 2 [86bd2159-9263-434f-8549-6e0e40e2259d] label='Evaluate'
Since Pankaj didn't disagree with any of Ana's explicit claims, we should start asking ourselves what Ana was *assuming*, because maybe Pankaj is disagreeing with an assumption.

The connection between Ana's two sentences assumes that "the proposed smoking ban *is trying to prevent people from doing things that harm only themselves*".

Does Pankaj disagree with this?

Yes! He's saying that people are still allowed to smoke all they want in private (it's not preventing people from doing things that harm only themselves). The ban applies only to smoking public spaces (so it's aimed at preventing people from doing things that harm *others*, in this case preventing cigarette smokers from harming others with their secondhand smoke).
-- step 3 [4f316b8c-be35-4d13-8f21-b59ae16185c2] label='Goal'
Look for something like "Whether the ban is preventing people from doing things that harm only themselves".
-- note A [e6e3674d-1b5b-4e8a-bab7-82c680153aa8]
As we analyzed, Pankaj makes no statements about the government at all.
-- note B [0ac01bec-dc9d-45df-bbe7-cc2615ebf4af]
As we analyzed, Pankaj makes no statements about the government at all.
-- note C [c40a32c3-0ce4-4b22-a830-6fc26f568985]
Ana would agree with this. It's implied by her statements that she opposes the ban because she thinks it's intended to prevent harm only to smokers themselves and she rejects that on libertarian principles.

Panjak would disagree with this, because by pointing out that you are banned from smoking in public places but not in private, he's implying that the ban is targeting the effects of smoking on others not on the smoker him/herself.
-- note D [c9c7d3fd-6a1d-4bbb-a8c5-5d180dd7bb40]
Pankaj would clearly agree with this because he explicitly says this. Can we derive from Ana's paragraph that she thinks that "the proposed ban would not prohibit smoking in public places"?

No, we can't derive that. She may think that the smoking ban applies to public places (as well as private places).

If we changed this answer to say "the proposed ban would prohibit smoking only in public places", then we could probably pick it.
-- note E [05b3361f-7bf0-4d38-be89-b3ff19bec065]
As we analyzed, Pankaj makes no statements about the government at all.

QID PT151-S2-Q18 | type Strengthen | key A
BANK STIM: In grasslands near the Namib Desert there are “fairy
circles”—large, circular patches that are entirely devoid
of vegetation. Since sand termite colonies were found in
every fairy circle they investigated, scientists hypothesize
that it is the burrowing activities of these termites that
cause the circles to form.
BANK STEM: Which one of the following, if true, most supports the
scientists’ hypothesis?
  A: Dying grass plants within newly forming fairy circles are damaged only at the roots.
  B: The grasses that grow around fairy circles are able to survive even the harshest and most prolonged droughts in the region.
  C: The soil in fairy circles typically has higher water content than the soil in areas immediately outside the circles.
  D: Fairy circles tend to form in areas that already have numerous other fairy circles.
  E: Species of animals that feed on sand termites are often found living near fairy circles.
BANK KEY: A
TAGS: {'A': 'Correct', 'B': 'Opposite', 'C': 'No Impact', 'D': 'No Impact', 'E': 'No Impact'}
