QID PT157-S3-Q25 | type Weaken | key A
BANK STIM: To test the claim that vitamin C is effective in treating
 acne, scientists administered it to one group of subjects
 and a placebo to a control group. The group receiving
 vitamin C had less severe acne during the study than
 did the control group. It was subsequently discovered,
 however, that half of the subjects in each group knew
 which kind of pill they were given. Among those who
 could not tell, no difference in the severity of acne
 was found between the two groups. Therefore, we can
 tentatively conclude that vitamin C has no real benefit in
 reducing the severity of acne.
BANK STEM: Which one of the following, if true, most seriously
 weakens the argument?
  A: The subjects who were given vitamin C had a history of suffering from more severe acne than did the subjects receiving a placebo.
  B: None of the subjects who were given vitamin C took additional doses of vitamin C on their own.
  C: During the study, the severity of the subjects’ acne was lower than the national average.
  D: Some of the subjects who were given placebos consumed foods during the study that are naturally rich in vitamin C.
  E: Some of the subjects who knew their pills were placebos did not actually take the pills they were given.
BANK KEY: A
TAGS: {'A': 'Correct', 'B': 'No Impact', 'C': 'No Impact', 'D': 'Strengthens', 'E': 'No Impact'}

=== STYLE standard 2ff83bdd-29a8-4a08-b301-adfd45fc3305
-- step 0 [292210ee-d1bf-40ad-997e-4dc382422946] label='Opposing Evidence'
During a study, a group of subjects receiving vitamin C had less severe acne than did the control group, which received a placebo.
-- step 1 [4e81365a-dec1-4b4a-8d89-8bb723eafd48] label='Evidence'
Half of the subjects in each group knew which kind of pill they were given. Among those who could not tell [which pill they were given], no difference in the severity of acne was found between the two groups.
-- step 2 [b9aec86e-0805-4623-8fce-3681bdb8332f] label='Conclusion'
We can tentatively conclude that vitamin C has no real benefit in reducing the severity of acne.
-- step 3 [5b90d24d-3bc5-4de9-9096-fbd5e6b4df0d] label='Evaluate'
Let's make sure that we're clear about what happened to the different groups that are compared in this study. We know that some subjects who received vitamin C had less severe acne than those who received a placebo. Among the subjects who didn't know which kind of pill they were given, there was no difference in the severity of acne. This means that the subjects who received vitamin C and also had less severe acne all knew that they were taking vitamin C, not a placebo. At first, the study seems to show a correlation between taking vitamin C and a reduction in acne. That's evidence—not proof, but evidence—that vitamin C reduces acne. But wait! There's a problem with the study. If people knew which pill they were taking, that could affect the results somehow. From this the argument concludes—at least tentatively—that vitamin C does not help reduce the severity of acne. This looks like an "unproven vs. proven false" flaw. Because this one particular study had a problem, it hasn't necessarily proven that vitamin C helps treat acne. But it's still possible that vitamin C does help treat acne, even if this one study hasn't proven that it does. We can't jump from the details of this one study to a general claim about the benefits of vitamin C.
-- step 4 [3f4a73c5-614b-4bf2-ab31-81985b672320] label='Goal'
A correct answer might provide evidence that the vitamin C actually did help to reduce subjects' acne, in spite of the apparent problem with the way the study was conducted.
-- note A [333af76a-9d2e-463a-89b1-af6de01cb2d9]
If the vitamin C subjects started with more severe acne than the placebo subjects, then the study results look very different. The vitamin C group had less severe acne during the study despite starting with worse acne — suggesting vitamin C actually reduced their acne enough to surpass the control group. Even among those who did not know which pill they received, having "no difference" in severity is significant if the vitamin C subgroup began with worse acne. Equal outcomes from unequal starting points suggest the treatment was effective. This undermines the conclusion that vitamin C provides no real benefit.
-- note B [8746afd0-c226-4735-9ab3-3898726ee7d9]
This answer rules out a potential confounding factor — vitamin C subjects secretly taking extra vitamin C. But ruling out this possibility does not change the study's findings or the argument's reasoning. The study already showed the vitamin C group had less severe acne overall but no difference among subjects who did not know their pill. Whether anyone took extra doses does not affect this comparison. Even if they did take extra, we would need to know whether those subjects were in the knowing or unknowing subgroup to evaluate the impact.
-- note C [b14f1e1a-4a4f-4b57-a126-4cda19b76316]
Whether all subjects had milder acne than the national average is irrelevant to the argument. The argument compares groups within the study — vitamin C versus placebo, knowing versus unknowing. External comparisons to the national average do not affect whether vitamin C was responsible for the differences observed within the study. Both groups could have below-average acne and the argument's reasoning would be unchanged.
-- note D [011c47e1-9daa-4d67-8a2d-7bff9623600d]
If placebo subjects consumed foods naturally rich in vitamin C, this muddies the distinction between the two groups. The control group was effectively getting some vitamin C through diet, which could have reduced their acne. If the placebo group's acne was already being reduced by dietary vitamin C, then the absence of a difference between groups does not prove vitamin C is ineffective — it suggests both groups were receiving some vitamin C. However, this actually supports the argument's conclusion more than it weakens it: if both groups got vitamin C and there was no difference, then additional vitamin C through pills provides no extra benefit.
-- note E [64024504-9bfd-41b6-ba6e-b7c4ad66198d]
Some subjects who knew they had placebos did not take them. This does not meaningfully affect the argument. Those subjects are in the placebo group regardless — they were not receiving vitamin C whether or not they swallowed their placebo pills. A placebo contains no active ingredient, so not taking it produces the same pharmacological result as taking it. At most, this introduces a minor methodological concern, but it does not provide any evidence that vitamin C actually reduces acne severity.

=== STYLE brief 882d0369-ee56-4170-93df-3b78be87975c
-- step 0 [9b6aea36-24df-472c-80a5-15eff79902d8] label='Conclusion'
Vitamin C has no real benefit in reducing acne severity.
-- step 1 [4c67282e-be1c-462e-a7c7-bc469df7a548] label='Evidence'
In a study, the vitamin C group had less severe acne. But half the subjects in each group knew which pill they received. Among those who could not tell, there was no difference in acne severity.
-- step 2 [2158cf8a-cd04-4a38-abe4-0000d9e81d46] label='Evaluate'
The argument attributes the initial positive results to a placebo-like effect — subjects who knew they were taking vitamin C may have experienced psychological benefits. But this reasoning assumes the two groups started with comparable acne severity. If the vitamin C group had worse acne to begin with, the fact that their acne was less severe during the study is more impressive.
-- step 3 [2675c5b0-2af9-4060-8c7d-c91896cbf725] label='Goal'
Find an answer that undermines the conclusion by suggesting vitamin C actually did help, despite the study's complications.
-- note A [0fb167f9-f092-4338-be1a-38eb153e337b]
If the vitamin C group started with worse acne, then equal outcomes during the study suggest vitamin C actually helped. Equal results from unequal baselines undermines the no-benefit conclusion.
-- note B [c9aee88f-b6d5-426a-ba6a-f8fd16782491]
Ruling out extra vitamin C doses does not change the study's findings. The comparison between knowing and unknowing subjects is unaffected.
-- note C [0ad5031c-5dc2-4584-857a-02e2a3288bb4]
The national average is irrelevant. The argument compares groups within the study, not against external benchmarks.
-- note D [b4f8ee68-7f98-489f-9339-ff0242629f75]
If the placebo group also got vitamin C through food, the lack of difference between groups still suggests the pills added no benefit. This supports the conclusion rather than weakening it.
-- note E [617546fd-d75d-44ea-abcf-78c95de0be02]
Not taking a placebo has the same pharmacological effect as taking one — there is no active ingredient either way. This does not affect the vitamin C comparison.

=== STYLE another_way b378e1b0-88e3-450f-9bdf-10644a048ea1
-- step 0 [57386fbd-6e0e-46c7-afc1-8bd1d1b7a07c] label='Conclusion'
Vitamin C does not actually help with acne. The study's positive results were all in people's heads.
-- step 1 [d854f4dc-2564-419c-a788-427af2eedb5f] label='Evidence'
The vitamin C group did better overall, but half the subjects knew what they were taking. When you look only at the people who did not know, there was no difference. So the argument chalks it up to placebo effect.
-- step 2 [db40efd3-ffae-49fe-9055-a2e458339552] label='Evaluate'
The argument makes a sneaky assumption: that both groups started with equally bad acne. But what if the vitamin C group had worse acne from the start? Then ending up at the same level as the control group would actually be evidence that vitamin C works — it just brought them down to baseline. The study's design flaw does not automatically mean the vitamin C did nothing.
-- step 3 [5bf9cae1-f204-4800-8f0e-f94c97ae5c6c] label='Goal'
Find the answer that gives a reason to believe vitamin C actually helped, despite the messy study design.
-- note A [6365e9c4-dfe1-4876-931f-8d668bf623b9]
Plot twist: the vitamin C group started with worse acne. That changes everything. The study found "no difference" between unknowing subjects — but if the vitamin C people had worse acne going in and ended up at the same level, that is evidence the vitamin C worked. It pulled them up to even. The argument assumed everyone started at the same place. This answer reveals they did not, and suddenly "no difference" looks like evidence of a treatment effect rather than evidence against one.
-- note B [6fd6bea2-0a2a-4efb-84e5-d29d46447b49]
Nobody snuck extra vitamin C. Okay. That just means the study ran as designed — everyone got what they were supposed to get and nothing more. The argument's logic is about why the benefit only showed up in people who knew they were taking vitamin C. This answer does not touch that question at all.
-- note C [e003e329-7bf0-46e7-8004-1b952dc9238b]
Everyone in the study had better skin than average? Good for them. But the argument is about whether vitamin C caused the difference between groups in the study, not about how everyone compared to the general population. You could run this study on a group of supermodels and the internal comparison would work the same way.
-- note D [fa758a01-be00-4123-be04-47a3fdc10084]
The placebo group was eating vitamin C-rich foods? If anything, that helps the argument. If both groups were getting vitamin C (one through pills, one through diet) and there was no difference, that is more evidence vitamin C does not help with acne. This answer accidentally supports the conclusion instead of weakening it.
-- note E [91cad954-b8d3-4ed8-95c7-28befcdfb826]
They did not take their sugar pills. So they got zero active ingredient instead of zero active ingredient. Nothing changed. A placebo is a placebo whether it is in your stomach or in your pocket. This does not provide any reason to believe vitamin C helps with acne, which is what would be needed to weaken the argument.

QID PT158-S2-Q16 | type Paradox | key C
BANK STIM: Historians have found that significant intellectual
 advances generally occur in cities or countries that have
 a stable political system. But these same historians
 acknowledge that in ancient Athens, Plato and Aristotle
 made unparalleled intellectual progress during a period of
 great political and social unrest.
BANK STEM: Which one of the following, if true, most helps to
 reconcile the historians’ apparent counterexample with
 their general rule?
  A: The political systems that have emerged since the time of Plato and Aristotle have in various ways been different from the political system in ancient Athens.
  B: The citizens of ancient Athens generally held in high esteem people who were accomplished intellectually.
  C: Financial support for intellectual endeavors is typically unavailable in unstable political environments, but in ancient Athens such support was provided by wealthy citizens.
  D: Significant intellectual advances sometimes, though not always, lead to stable political environments.
  E: Many thinkers besides Plato and Aristotle contributed to the intellectual achievements of ancient Athens.
BANK KEY: C
TAGS: {'A': 'No Impact', 'B': 'No Impact', 'C': 'Correct', 'D': 'No Impact', 'E': 'No Impact'}

=== STYLE standard 0e1705d8-633d-477e-8cc7-d9923bcd514e
-- step 0 [6a3471d4-e4cb-4d4a-be52-430dc422c645] label='Given that ...'
Significant intellectual advances generally occur in cities or countries that have a stable political system.
-- step 1 [d8c75ac0-24dd-45c0-9300-f35a33ea9c70] label='How can it be that ...'
Plato and Aristotle made unparalleled intellectual progress during a period of great political and social unrest?
-- step 2 [fd7cc7f3-0c52-4d37-aaf8-7873a2088d60] label='Evaluate / Goal'
This isn't really paradoxical, since the general rule in the first sentence is only saying that intellectual advances generally occur in stable political systems. That's not saying that advances only occur in stable political systems. So the fact that Plato and Aristotle are examples of advances that occurred in unstable political systems isn't really contradicting the general rule. It's just an exception. So we should probably understand our task as, "how come Plato and Aristotle were able to make their intellectual advances, despite the unstable political system?" (Maybe they were well-insulated from instability and so within the world of Plato's Academy it still felt like a stable political system)
-- note A [8d97cdea-36ea-4dd2-95b4-bd89a455d48e]
Knowing that modern political systems vary from those of Plato's time does not help explain how Plato and Aristotle achieved intellectual advances during political instability. This answer provides a general historical observation about the evolution of political systems without identifying any specific mechanism that would reconcile the counterexample with the rule. The paradox asks: why did instability not prevent intellectual progress in Athens? The fact that political systems have changed since then offers no insight into what feature of ancient Athens allowed intellectual work to flourish despite turmoil. It is background information that fails to address the specific puzzle.
-- note B [5b963036-0097-4c78-a61f-1aeaa43de10b]
The citizens of Athens holding intellectuals in high esteem explains why intellectual work might have been socially rewarded, but it does not address the specific relationship between political instability and intellectual output. The general rule is that instability prevents intellectual advances — presumably because instability disrupts the conditions that make intellectual work possible (funding, safety, institutional support). Social esteem for intellectuals is a separate factor that does not address the mechanism by which instability normally blocks progress. Athens could have revered its thinkers while still having conditions that should have prevented their success. The paradox requires an explanation of how the usual barriers were circumvented, not merely evidence that intellectuals were popular.
-- note C [114e83fd-ab48-4085-a287-8e874670d1ac]
This answer resolves the paradox by identifying the specific mechanism through which political instability normally prevents intellectual advances: the loss of financial support. If instability typically eliminates funding for intellectual work, then the general rule holds because scholars cannot pursue their research without resources. The key phrase is "typically" — Plato and Aristotle were exceptions. Plato came from one of Athens' wealthiest families, and Aristotle was supported by powerful patrons (eventually including Philip of Macedon). Their personal wealth and patronage meant they did not depend on the kind of institutional or governmental funding that political instability would have disrupted. The general rule remains valid: instability normally defunds intellectual work. The counterexample is explained: Plato and Aristotle had independent financial means that insulated them from the usual economic consequences of political turmoil. Both facts coexist perfectly.
-- note D [8e0e9495-513d-4b9a-9400-ac34ec7c33eb]
The idea that intellectual advances sometimes lead to political stability reverses the direction of the relationship without resolving the paradox. The general rule says stability enables intellectual advances (stability causes progress). This answer says intellectual advances sometimes cause stability (progress causes stability). Even if both directions are sometimes true, this does not explain how Plato and Aristotle achieved their advances during instability. If anything, this answer raises a new puzzle: if intellectual advances can produce stability, why was Athens still unstable despite hosting Plato and Aristotle? The answer addresses a different causal direction from what the paradox requires and leaves the original puzzle untouched.
-- note E [d7fec877-56bb-4d30-b311-5ae90dd30d8e]
The fact that other thinkers besides Plato and Aristotle contributed to Athenian intellectual achievement broadens the counterexample rather than resolving it. The paradox is that major intellectual advances occurred during instability. Knowing that more people participated in those advances does not explain how any of them — Plato, Aristotle, or others — managed to succeed despite political turmoil. If anything, this makes the counterexample more robust: it was not just two exceptional individuals defying the odds, but an entire intellectual community thriving during chaos. The paradox gets bigger, not smaller. A resolution needs to explain the mechanism that allowed intellectual work to proceed despite instability, not expand the list of people who accomplished it.

=== STYLE brief 101d8415-d97b-40aa-a53f-d20fe9d413db
-- step 0 [bcd0589e-de0d-4a65-8c88-3983019abfcf] label='The Paradox'
Historians find that significant intellectual advances generally require political stability. Yet Plato and Aristotle achieved unparalleled intellectual progress during politically and socially unstable ancient Athens.
-- step 1 [317191dc-c217-4184-8058-599520d8b55d] label='Goal'
Find an answer that explains how Plato and Aristotle could have succeeded despite instability — reconciling the counterexample with the general rule rather than eliminating either one.
-- note A [a595973e-51c2-4333-9cf7-04bfc2edbe80]
Changes in political systems since ancient Greece do not explain how Athens produced intellectual advances during instability. This is irrelevant historical context that does not address the paradox.
-- note B [15d18cd0-eb90-4693-8e25-f7aab4760f5f]
Social esteem for intellectuals does not explain how instability's usual barriers to intellectual progress were overcome. Being respected does not neutralize the specific obstacles that political turmoil creates.
-- note C [f76218c3-1d77-47c4-af4e-9d4ede9da4d4]
If instability normally blocks intellectual work by cutting off financial support, Plato and Aristotle are explained as exceptions who had independent wealth. The general rule holds (instability defunds scholarship), and the counterexample is resolved (they had personal resources).
-- note D [f1119e26-f504-4a06-931c-9d733a6e85b8]
Intellectual advances sometimes causing stability reverses the causal direction without explaining the paradox. The question is how advances happen despite instability, not whether advances produce stability.
-- note E [5bcdf452-19a4-4f29-90cb-2bc3ceb1508b]
More thinkers contributing to Athenian achievements expands the counterexample rather than resolving it. The question is how intellectual progress occurred during instability, not how many people were involved.

=== STYLE another_way 83ba148f-e2e8-4450-a45f-23405986edb1
-- step 0 [692d31fc-5e46-4a7e-920c-ee718b9dc7da] label='The Paradox'
Historians say intellectual breakthroughs happen when things are politically calm. Then they turn around and acknowledge that Plato and Aristotle — arguably the greatest intellectual duo in human history — did their best work while Athens was in political chaos. That is like a nutritionist saying candy is terrible for you while admitting that the healthiest person they have ever met eats nothing but Skittles.
-- step 1 [8ff67d99-0060-46e2-8110-0a67915c5cf7] label='Goal'
Find the answer that explains how Plato and Aristotle pulled off their intellectual achievements despite the political turmoil — without destroying the general rule or denying the facts of history.
-- note A [0656437b-84b4-4517-8e5d-0e2c71fc13ad]
"Political systems have changed over time." Thank you, every history textbook ever written. But how does that explain why Plato and Aristotle thrived in chaos? It does not. Saying that politics is different now is like explaining why someone survived a plane crash by pointing out that planes have changed since the Wright Brothers. True? Sure. Helpful? Not remotely.
-- note B [e56496bb-fdae-4db0-8c85-7cf09961477b]
Athens thought smart people were cool. Great. But giving thinkers a standing ovation does not explain how they managed to think straight while the government was falling apart. The question is about the mechanism of how instability blocks intellectual work — and mere popularity does not remove any of those barriers. Imagine a scientist trying to do research during a civil war. "But everyone loves scientists!" they cry, as the lab gets burned down. Esteem is nice, but it does not fix the underlying problem.
-- note C [4c0009fb-f2c9-4d92-b760-afa4a8affb61]
The general rule says instability kills intellectual progress. This answer reveals the murder weapon: money. Political chaos typically drains the funding that scholars need to, well, scholar. But Plato and Aristotle were not exactly applying for government grants. Plato was born into one of Athens' wealthiest families, and Aristotle had a Macedonian king bankrolling his research. They were the ancient equivalent of independently wealthy hobbyists who could keep working even when the economy tanked. So the rule still works — instability kills funding, and no funding means no intellectual progress. Plato and Aristotle just had their own money, making them bulletproof to the usual financial consequences of political turmoil. Mystery solved.
-- note D [e420980c-5b0c-4564-94d5-69de18d5c269]
This answer is running the argument in reverse — like saying "eating dinner sometimes causes hunger" when someone asked why you can be hungry before dinner. The paradox asks: how can intellectual progress happen without stability? This answer says: intellectual progress sometimes creates stability. Those are two completely different questions. And it raises a new problem: if smart people stabilize politics, why was Athens still a mess with Plato and Aristotle around? This answer makes things more confusing, not less.
-- note E [d1eb4cad-fa1e-44d5-8687-f3c0e8bc249f]
"Actually, there were even MORE geniuses thriving during the chaos!" Great — so the counterexample is even bigger than we thought. Instead of explaining how two people defied the rule, now there is a whole crowd of overachievers to account for. This answer is like a detective trying to solve one mysterious disappearance and discovering that actually twenty people vanished. The mystery deepens, not resolves. The paradox needed an explanation, and this answer handed it a megaphone instead.

QID PT158-S2-Q18 | type Flaw | key E
BANK STIM: Records reveal that of physical therapy patients who
 received less than six weeks of treatment, about 31
 percent showed major improvement, regardless of
 whether they were treated by a general practitioner or by
 a specialist. Of patients who received physical therapy
 for a longer time, again regardless of whether they were
 treated by a general practitioner or by a specialist, about
 50 percent showed major improvement. Therefore,
 the choice between seeing a specialist or a general
 practitioner for necessary physical therapy will not affect
 one’s chances of major improvement.
BANK STEM: The reasoning in the argument is most vulnerable to
 criticism on the grounds that the argument
  A: presumes, without providing justification, that effectiveness of different practitioners in bringing about major improvement cannot differ at all if their effectiveness in bringing about any improvement does not differ
  B: provides no information about the kinds of injuries that require short-term as opposed to long-term treatment
  C: overlooks the possibility that patients are more strongly biased to report favorably on one of the two types of medical professionals than on the other
  D: fails to indicate whether the number of patients surveyed who saw a general practitioner was equal to the number who saw a specialist
  E: overlooks the possibility that specialists and general practitioners each tend to excel at treating a different type of injury
BANK KEY: E
TAGS: {'A': 'Not Assumed', 'B': 'Not an Objection', 'C': 'Not an Objection', 'D': 'Not Assumed', 'E': 'Correct'}

=== STYLE standard 9dc92652-6d3d-474d-b725-2d5369d40bbd
-- step 0 [fdd102c0-8e90-4ab3-9b95-383d7ed5d2b1] label='Conclusion (therefore)'
The choice between seeing a specialist or a general practitioner for needed PT will not affect one's chances of major improvement.
-- step 1 [3763d81a-64c3-4fb9-ae0a-a4bc3c7a30cc] label='Evidence'
Among people who received less than six weeks of PT, 31% showed major improvement, whether they were treated by a general practitioner or a by a specialist. Among people who received more PT, again about 50% showed major improvement, regardless of whether they were treated by a general practitioner or by a specialist.
-- step 2 [cb8adb6d-d0e9-4940-b968-8cdcd4c6227b] label='Evaluate'
This conclusion is essentially anti-causal, saying it doesn't make a difference whether you're assigned to a general practitioner or to a specialist. If we were going to argue that it does make a difference which one you're assigned to, then how are we going to explain why people seem to be getting the same rate of major improvement regardless of which type of PT they see? There is a tiny detail that crops up in the conclusion about "necessary physical therapy". The evidence didn't say that the people receiving physical therapy were getting needed physical therapy. Maybe when it's needed, it really makes a difference whether you see a GP vs. a specialist, but the records are distorted by the fact that tons of people get PT when they don't need it. Maybe we're currently doing a great job of assigning people to a GP vs. a specialist based on what their specific situation is, and if we stopped caring about which type of therapist we assigned patients, the 31% and 50% marks would go way down. Conversely, maybe we're currently not doing a great job of assigning people to a GP vs. a specialist based on their specific situation, and if we started caring about which type we assigned patients to, we would do better than 31% and 50% of patients showing major improvement.
-- step 3 [db1f7ed4-5792-4923-90d8-a4c6f39f24ae] label='Goal'
The author is assuming that if both types of therapist are getting the same percent of their patients to show major improvement, then it doesn't matter whether a patient sees one or the other (for the sake of major improvement). But the author is failing to consider that those current rates of major improvement might change were we to concentrate more or concentrate less on caring about whether patients saw a specialist vs. GP based on their specific needs.
-- note A [25055e28-603e-41ba-a038-2e99a1c467d8]
The argument does not presume that effectiveness should be measured solely by improvement rates. In fact, improvement rate is a reasonable metric for evaluating treatment effectiveness. The argument's flaw is not the choice of metric but the failure to control for confounding variables within that metric. Even if we accept improvement rate as the right measure, the comparison is still flawed because it does not account for differences in the types of conditions treated by specialists versus generalists. This answer misidentifies the problem: the measurement itself is fine; the comparison methodology is what fails. Two practitioners can both be measured by improvement rate, and the comparison can still be meaningless if they are treating fundamentally different patient populations.
-- note B [d55ccc72-2bc7-40b2-b69f-5b0b79b48d53]
Knowing which kinds of injuries require short-term versus long-term treatment is unnecessary for the argument's conclusion. The argument groups patients by duration and compares outcomes within each group. Whether back injuries need six weeks and knee injuries need twelve is irrelevant to the question of whether practitioner type affects outcomes. The argument's flaw is about who treats what types of patients, not about which injuries require which treatment durations. This answer points to missing information that would be useful for a different analysis but does not identify the logical error in the argument as presented.
-- note C [50ac8dfa-b0b7-402f-af44-9c90e7836fcf]
Patient reporting bias — whether patients are more favorable toward one type of practitioner — is a valid methodological concern in general, but it is not the specific flaw in this argument. The argument assumes the reported improvement rates are accurate and draws a flawed conclusion from them. Even if the data is perfectly unbiased, the comparison fails because it does not control for differences in the patient populations seen by each type of practitioner. Introducing a reporting bias concern shifts attention from the argument's actual logical error (comparing non-equivalent groups) to a hypothetical data quality issue that, even if resolved, would not fix the underlying problem.
-- note D [d719533f-d6de-486c-9cd6-90c1a21059da]
Whether the number of patients surveyed was equal between groups is not the argument's flaw. Statistical comparisons of percentages do not require equal sample sizes — the percentages are already normalized. The argument compares 31% to 31% and 50% to 50%; these percentages can be equally meaningful whether derived from 100 patients or 10,000. The flaw is that equal percentages from different patient populations are being treated as evidence of equal effectiveness. If specialists treat harder cases, equal improvement rates actually indicate superior performance by specialists. The sample size question is a statistical concern that is separate from and less fundamental than the confounding-variable problem that actually undermines the argument.
-- note E [5cdd5e95-22eb-4381-8fdc-7e784071f139]
This answer identifies the argument's central flaw: it compares raw improvement rates without accounting for the possibility that specialists and generalists treat fundamentally different types of conditions. If specialists tend to handle complex, difficult cases (torn ligaments, post-surgical rehabilitation, chronic conditions) while generalists handle simpler problems (mild strains, basic mobility issues), then equal improvement rates actually indicate that specialists are more effective — they are achieving the same results with harder cases. Conversely, if generalists excel at treating certain conditions and specialists at others, a patient's choice of practitioner absolutely matters, even though the aggregate numbers look identical. The argument committed the ecological fallacy: drawing individual-level conclusions from group-level data that masks crucial subgroup differences. By overlooking that each practitioner type might have a comparative advantage with different conditions, the argument prematurely concluded that the choice does not matter.

=== STYLE brief ac973e19-4a31-4aec-9149-7d02d5240926
-- step 0 [d664ee75-61ce-4a89-9be5-099402e64d62] label='Conclusion'
The choice between a specialist and a general practitioner for physical therapy will not affect one's chances of major improvement.
-- step 1 [dc36e323-f569-4d4b-8d52-4b4c5f0b6ca6] label='Evidence'
Patients treated for less than six weeks showed 31% improvement regardless of practitioner type. Patients treated for longer showed 50% improvement regardless of practitioner type.
-- step 2 [daca0093-fc71-4bdb-8431-80cedf488ab7] label='Evaluate'
The data groups patients by treatment duration and shows equal rates within each group. But this ignores the possibility that specialists and generalists treat different types of patients. If specialists handle harder cases and generalists handle easier ones, equal raw percentages could mask a real advantage for one type of practitioner.
-- step 3 [fa125bea-b554-4d2a-9274-7059329b8fb7] label='Goal'
Find the flaw: the argument overlooks that the types of patients each practitioner sees might differ, making the raw comparison misleading.
-- note A [90139a90-8ba5-4b66-a692-d81783ce4d98]
Improvement rate is a reasonable metric. The flaw is not the measurement but the failure to control for different patient populations, making the comparison misleading despite using a valid metric.
-- note B [6137c951-45e9-42ff-a36d-37d7fd99d12f]
Which injuries require short versus long treatment is irrelevant. The flaw concerns whether specialists and generalists treat different conditions, not the duration-injury relationship.
-- note C [b8568d5a-566b-46d6-9a8d-2ef30dac4e76]
Reporting bias is a data quality concern, not the argument's flaw. Even with perfectly accurate data, the comparison is still flawed because it does not control for different patient populations.
-- note D [07bb61dc-16d4-4e2e-90be-67ee9c066ab7]
Equal sample sizes are not needed for comparing percentages. The flaw is that equal percentages from different patient populations do not prove equal effectiveness — a confounding variable issue, not a sample size issue.
-- note E [5f6d1ffc-bcf1-4ea8-a4f6-2d0eba1076c2]
If specialists treat harder cases and generalists treat easier ones, matching improvement rates actually show specialists are more effective. The argument ignored that different practitioner types may excel at different conditions, making the aggregate comparison misleading.

=== STYLE another_way 5cc6f141-6674-468b-968e-d3a883808eaf
-- step 0 [5619eca1-157f-40b8-bd70-876c8a18f004] label='Conclusion'
Does not matter whether you see a specialist or a general practitioner for physical therapy — your odds are the same either way.
-- step 1 [b6fe2d68-7bad-4169-8999-d501d47f5408] label='Evidence'
The numbers look identical: 31% improvement for short-term patients with either type of practitioner, 50% for long-term patients with either type. Case closed? Not quite.
-- step 2 [9688f8ca-bdfc-4ac2-8808-d339900829cf] label='Evaluate'
Imagine a heart surgeon and a family doctor both have a 90% patient survival rate. Sounds equal, right? But the heart surgeon is doing open-heart surgery while the family doctor is treating colds. Equal percentages mean nothing if the difficulty of the cases is completely different. The argument looked at matching numbers and declared a draw without asking: are these practitioners even treating the same kinds of patients? If specialists handle the tough cases and generalists handle the easy ones, the "equal" outcomes actually prove specialists are better, not that the choice does not matter.
-- step 3 [d1908f93-442e-48c2-b3c3-427d6b3a5a5f] label='Goal'
Spot the answer that calls out this apples-to-oranges comparison — the possibility that different practitioners treat different types of conditions.
-- note A [97c6e92f-ce23-458c-b173-8443c249b3d2]
There is nothing wrong with using improvement rates to measure how well a treatment works — that is actually a pretty sensible approach. The problem is not the ruler; it is that the argument measured two completely different things and declared them equal. Imagine weighing a bowling ball and a watermelon and, finding they weigh the same, declaring them identical objects. The scale is fine. The comparison is the problem.
-- note B [eb2b5cca-2e23-459f-be64-974d78d097d4]
Sure, it would be interesting to know which injuries need a quick fix versus a long haul. But that is not the argument's problem. The argument's problem is that it compared two practitioners without checking if they were treating the same kinds of patients. Knowing the duration-injury connection is like asking what color the walls are when the building's foundation is cracked — interesting detail, wrong problem.
-- note C [70e4c383-b7ee-4fe4-8d5a-085066a94feb]
Are patients fudging their reviews? Maybe, but that is a data accuracy problem, not the argument's logical problem. The argument could have the most honest, most accurate data on the planet and still be wrong, because it compared practitioners who might be treating completely different types of patients. Fixing the Yelp reviews does not fix the fundamental apples-to-oranges comparison. This answer is polishing the telescope while pointing it at the wrong star.
-- note D [1dd38c9f-5102-4ed9-9b59-f9d75b084803]
The "but was the sample size the same?" objection misses the forest for the trees. Percentages are already built to handle different group sizes — that is literally what percentages are for. The problem is not how many patients were counted but what kinds of patients were counted. If specialists treat broken spines while generalists treat sprained ankles, getting the same improvement percentage is not evidence of equal skill. It is evidence that the specialist is performing miracles while the generalist is handling routine cases. The sizes of the groups are a footnote; the composition of the groups is the headline.
-- note E [6a3e3475-754e-48bb-b0b7-59a02a0c9479]
Picture this: a brain surgeon and a dermatologist both have a 95% success rate. Equal, right? The brain surgeon is removing tumors and the dermatologist is treating acne. Those identical numbers tell a very different story once you know what each doctor is actually treating. The argument did exactly this — it saw matching percentages for specialists and generalists and declared them equally effective without asking the obvious question: are they treating the same kinds of patients? If the specialist is rehabilitating complex post-surgical cases while the generalist is stretching out someone with a stiff neck, matching numbers mean the specialist is crushing it. The argument looked at the scoreboard without checking who was playing which opponent.

QID PT158-S3-Q20 | type Must Be True | key B
BANK STIM: The study of primates is interesting for many reasons,
 including the fact that only primates have opposable
 thumbs. The lemurs are lower primates and the only
 primates indigenous to Madagascar, a large island off the
 coast of southeastern Africa. Some species of lemurs are
 the only living lower primates that are diurnal—that is,
 active primarily during the day. All higher primates are
 thought to have evolved from a single diurnal species of
 lower primates.
BANK STEM: Which one of the following can be properly inferred
 from the information above?
  A: The chimpanzee, a higher primate, evolved from the lemur.
  B: No primates indigenous to Madagascar are diurnal higher primates.
  C: No higher primate is nocturnal.
  D: There are some lemurs without opposable thumbs.
  E: There are no nocturnal lemurs.
BANK KEY: B
TAGS: {'A': 'Supported But Not Certain', 'B': 'Correct', 'C': 'Too Strong', 'D': 'No Support', 'E': 'Too Strong'}

=== STYLE standard 873984b0-9ac3-4380-b85b-6f45db1b1d8b
-- step 0 [305069f9-4d24-4a4c-a055-1c1eeed9cd2c] label='Information'
```
conditionals (only / the only / all)
have opposable thumbs → primate
primate indigenous → lemurs
to Madagascar
lower primates and → certain lemurs
diurnal and living
higher primate → evolved from one single
diurnal lower primate
```
-- step 1 [82c5e9d7-930d-43d7-8f41-8564cd2d04a3] label='Evaluate'
We're trying to figure out whether we can combine any of these claims. We got multiple conditionals, but it doesn't seem like any of them chain together. What are the Overlapping Concepts? Lots. Primates, lower primates, lemurs, and diurnal all show up multiple times. Given that we were told that all higher primates evolved from a single diurnal species of lower primates, and given that some species of lemur are the only living lower primates that are diurnal, we have some support for the inference that all higher primates evolved from a lemur species, but this is Must Be True, not Most Supported. We don't know that for sure.
-- step 2 [ce66f17f-5b51-4866-8b4a-fcdbf9284a50] label='Goal'
Look for a provable answer. It's hard to predict what it will be since this assortment of facts didn't lend itself to any obvious combining / synthesizing.
-- note A [d09bbec0-51eb-49df-89af-535df224ddeb]
The passage states that all higher primates evolved from a single diurnal lower primate species, but it never identifies that species as a lemur. The passage says some lemurs are diurnal lower primates, and higher primates evolved from a diurnal lower primate — but these could be different species of diurnal lower primates. The passage does not establish that the ancestral species was a lemur, let alone a chimpanzee's specific ancestor. Furthermore, "evolved from" describes a distant ancestral relationship, not a direct lineage claim linking chimpanzees specifically to lemurs. This inference requires assumptions beyond what the passage states.
-- note B [d64c48e3-e2d5-4ae0-991c-bb04e630db40]
The passage states that lemurs are the only primates indigenous to Madagascar (F3) and that lemurs are lower primates (F2). From these two facts, it follows necessarily that all primates indigenous to Madagascar are lower primates — and therefore no primates indigenous to Madagascar are higher primates. Since no higher primates are indigenous to Madagascar, no diurnal higher primates are indigenous to Madagascar either. This answer captures exactly this inference: "no primates indigenous to Madagascar are diurnal higher primates." The reasoning is airtight — if the entire set (higher primates) is excluded from Madagascar, then any subset of that set (diurnal higher primates) is also excluded. No additional assumptions are needed.
-- note C [e667bfa1-7239-4f50-87cf-0a48170a4d22]
The passage says all higher primates are thought to have evolved from a single diurnal species of lower primates. This tells us the ancestral species was diurnal, but it does not tell us that all current higher primates are diurnal. Evolution from a diurnal ancestor does not guarantee that all descendants remain diurnal. Many higher primate species could have evolved nocturnal habits over millions of years. In fact, some higher primates (like certain prosimians reclassified or owl monkeys) are nocturnal. The passage provides no information about the current activity patterns of higher primates as a group — only about their evolutionary origin.
-- note D [4078470b-e989-4112-a78d-0fb9d5f62cc4]
The passage states that only primates have opposable thumbs — meaning ALL primates have them, and nothing else does. This means lemurs, as primates, have opposable thumbs. The passage does not say or imply that any lemurs lack opposable thumbs. The "only primates" formulation establishes that opposable thumbs are exclusive to primates, but it does not address variation within the primate order. Since lemurs are primates, the passage actually implies they DO have opposable thumbs, making this answer contradict the passage rather than follow from it.
-- note E [38a47ea5-e7dc-41b3-9346-aa7cfd5955c0]
The passage says some species of lemurs are the only living lower primates that are diurnal. This means some lemur species are diurnal, but it does not say all lemurs are diurnal. The word "some" leaves open the possibility that other lemur species are nocturnal. In fact, many lemur species in the real world are nocturnal, and the passage's careful use of "some species of lemurs" rather than "all lemurs" signals that not every lemur is diurnal. This answer claims there are no nocturnal lemurs, which goes beyond — and likely contradicts — the passage's careful "some" qualifier.

=== STYLE brief b5e295e6-132a-45de-9427-67850ac74596

Lemurs are the only primates indigenous to Madagascar. Lemurs are lower primates. Some lemur species are the only diurnal lower primates. All higher primates evolved from a single diurnal lower primate species.

Since lemurs are the ONLY primates in Madagascar, and lemurs are lower primates, no higher primates are indigenous to Madagascar. Any primates in Madagascar — diurnal or not — are lower primates (lemurs).
-- step 2 [5a3a57f0-8eff-40c9-9421-0532f213cf49] label='Goal'
Find the inference that follows necessarily. The key deduction: no primates indigenous to Madagascar are diurnal higher primates, because no primates indigenous to Madagascar are higher primates at all.
-- note A [077c724e-b06d-474d-94fc-6bdf07d9f33e]
The passage never identifies the ancestral diurnal lower primate as a lemur. Higher primates evolved from "a single diurnal species of lower primates," but that species may not be a lemur.

Lemurs are the only primates in Madagascar, and lemurs are lower primates. So no higher primates are indigenous to Madagascar — and therefore no diurnal higher primates are either. This follows necessarily from the stated facts.
-- note C [010cc4b3-bb32-4fab-92db-ae3fc4561737]
Evolving from a diurnal ancestor does not guarantee all descendants are diurnal. The passage tells us about the ancestral species, not about the current activity patterns of all higher primates.

The passage says only primates have opposable thumbs. Since lemurs are primates, they have them. Nothing suggests any lemurs lack opposable thumbs.

=== STYLE another_way b57887d3-b0f8-4914-9946-cdd27c5968c0
-- step 0 [2aa66154-5e87-4938-b9e7-ac126e9421ce] label='Statements'
Lemurs are Madagascar's only primates, and lemurs are lower primates. Some lemurs are diurnal (day-active). Higher primates all evolved from some diurnal lower primate species. And only primates have opposable thumbs — a fun fact that mostly sits on the sidelines here.

The big inference: Madagascar has lemurs and only lemurs when it comes to primates. Lemurs are lower primates. So: zero higher primates are indigenous to Madagascar. That means no diurnal higher primates in Madagascar either — because there are no higher primates there, period. It is a simple subset argument: if no X exists in location Y, then no special type of X exists in location Y either.
-- step 2 [e97a8ab5-f5c1-4400-b0b1-64b620fe0a89] label='Goal'

Close, tempting, but a bridge too far for a "must be true" question.

Madagascar has lemurs and only lemurs when it comes to primates. Lemurs are lower primates. So there are zero higher primates in Madagascar — diurnal, nocturnal, or otherwise. You cannot find a diurnal higher primate in Madagascar because you cannot find ANY higher primate in Madagascar. It is like asking "are there any red sports cars in the parking lot?" when the parking lot is empty. No cars, no red sports cars. No higher primates, no diurnal higher primates. Simple, clean, and guaranteed by the facts.
-- note C [df438219-1f49-473f-97ef-419c08a43511]
Just because great-great-great-grandpa was a morning person does not mean every descendant wakes up at dawn. The passage says higher primates evolved from a diurnal species, not that they all stayed diurnal. Evolution is not a photocopier — traits change over millions of years. Some higher primates might have gone nocturnal along the way. The passage tells us where they came from, not where they all ended up. This answer treats ancestry as destiny, which is not how evolution works.
-- note D [4c57265c-53f7-4d19-9303-adf6bfe5111d]
The passage says "only primates have opposable thumbs" — which means if you are a primate, you have them. Lemurs are primates. Therefore, lemurs have opposable thumbs. This answer tries to claim some lemurs are missing them, which goes against the grain of everything the passage said. It is like being told "only birds have feathers" and concluding "some birds do not have feathers." The passage gave lemurs their opposable thumbs by including them in the primate club. This answer tries to take those thumbs away without any textual support.
-- note E [00dff09d-8234-48a2-81f7-057948292129]
The passage said "SOME species of lemurs" are diurnal. SOME. Not all. Not every single one. Some. That little word is doing a lot of work, and this answer steamrolls right over it. If every lemur were diurnal, the passage would not have bothered with "some." The fact that it specified "some species" strongly implies other lemur species are NOT diurnal — meaning nocturnal lemurs very likely exist. This answer says they do not, which contradicts the passage's own careful word choice.

QID PT158-S4-Q22 | type Weaken | key E
BANK STIM: Historian: Because medieval epistemology (theory of
 knowledge) is a complex subject, intellectual
 historians have, until recently, failed to produce
 a definition that would help to determine what
 should and what should not be included in
 it. Clearly, the solution is to define medieval
 epistemology simply as “the epistemological
 beliefs of the medieval epistemologists.” That
 way, if we want to know whether medieval
 epistemology includes some epistemological
 claim, we just ask whether any medieval
 epistemologists believed it. If any did, it is part
 of medieval epistemology; if any medieval
 epistemologists believed the opposite, then that
 opposite claim is part of medieval epistemology.
BANK STEM: Which one of the following, if true, would most weaken
 the historian’s argument?
  A: Medieval epistemologists held some of the same epistemological beliefs as did ancient epistemologists.
  B: The epistemological beliefs of medieval epistemologists depended upon their beliefs about nonepistemological matters.
  C: The writings of most medieval epistemologists include passages that are clearly not about epistemology.
  D: Some medieval epistemologists had epistemological beliefs that contradicted the epistemological beliefs of other medieval epistemologists.
  E: There is much debate as to which medieval thinkers, if any, were epistemologists.
BANK KEY: E
TAGS: {'A': 'Out of Scope', 'B': 'No Impact', 'C': 'No Impact', 'D': 'No Impact', 'E': 'Correct'}

=== STYLE standard bc99369b-8d3d-451e-9228-15efe84d9372
-- step 0 [b297870f-c027-4cf8-9986-89f93ab41452] label='Conclusion'
Medieval epistemology should be defined as "the epistemological beliefs of the medieval epistemologists."
-- step 1 [99121926-d49a-415e-a029-61f6329177ef] label='Evidence'
If any medieval epistemologists believed claim X, then we'll say "medieval epistemology believed X".
If any medieval epistemologists believed claim X was false, then we'll say "medieval epistemology did not believe X".
-- step 2 [92039a68-223d-42f3-9bd9-b5a531bf5340] label='Evaluate'
This definition only works if we can pick out the medieval epistemologists first. How would we know who they are? Presumably they're the thinkers who did medieval epistemology. But that's the very thing we're trying to define.
Notice what is NOT a problem for the author: disagreement. He already says that if one epistemologist believed X and another believed the opposite, both claims count. So contradictions among epistemologists don't hurt his plan.
-- step 3 [7d3ea25e-dd71-4ada-937c-ae04e2252a50] label='Goal'
Look for an answer that makes it hard to identify who the medieval epistemologists were. If we can't pick them out, the definition gives us nothing to work with.
Watch out for answers about epistemologists contradicting each other. The author built that into his plan, so it doesn't weaken it.
-- note A [3f5c68c4-8737-490f-b41b-1ca69027a118]
Whether medieval epistemologists shared beliefs with ancient epistemologists concerns the originality of their ideas, not the applicability of the proposed definition. Even if every belief they held was borrowed from antiquity, the definition still functions — you would identify the medieval epistemologists, catalog their beliefs, and end up with a medieval epistemology that mirrors the ancient version. Overlapping content between eras does not make the definition impossible to apply. This answer addresses the content of medieval epistemology, not the workability of the definition itself.
-- note B [01678eae-d0c8-4317-8851-790c8b1813a1]
This answer reveals that medieval epistemologists' beliefs were shaped by non-epistemological factors such as politics and religion. While historically illuminating, this does not challenge the proposed definition. The definition instructs us to identify medieval epistemologists and catalog their epistemological beliefs. The causes of those beliefs are irrelevant to this process. Even if every position they held was motivated by religious dogma rather than pure philosophical inquiry, those positions are still their epistemological beliefs. Knowing why they believed what they believed does not prevent us from identifying what they believed.
-- note C [2ab8be91-6574-4c5d-b0dc-d80bbe02a89e]
This answer points out that medieval epistemologists wrote about topics beyond epistemology, meaning their works contain mixed content. The implication is that applying the definition would require separating epistemological from non-epistemological material. But this is a manageable editorial task, not a fatal flaw. Scholars regularly distinguish between a thinker's epistemological claims and their ethical, metaphysical, or theological writings. The definition does not require that medieval epistemologists wrote only about epistemology — just that their epistemological beliefs can be identified within their broader work. The truly devastating weakness would be one showing the definition is logically impossible to apply, not merely that it requires careful reading.
-- note D [28029a1b-b551-46c4-93ea-97824d1d5775]
If medieval epistemologists held contradictory beliefs, the proposed definition would simply produce a body of thought containing internal disagreements. But this is entirely normal for any intellectual tradition. Modern epistemology, ethics, physics — every discipline contains competing viewpoints among its practitioners. The definition explicitly accounts for this: if one epistemologist believed X and another believed the opposite, both are included. Internal contradiction is a feature of the definition, not a bug. This answer describes a consequence the historian already anticipated and accepted, not a problem that undermines the definition's applicability.
-- note E [6ed8926a-96f2-4261-831b-8f6ed4b064c7]
This answer exposes the definition's fatal circularity. The definition says: medieval epistemology consists of the beliefs held by medieval epistemologists. To apply it, you must first identify who the medieval epistemologists were. But this answer reveals that there is significant scholarly debate about which medieval thinkers qualify as epistemologists. Resolving that debate requires a clear understanding of what medieval epistemology encompasses — the very thing the definition is supposed to provide. The result is a vicious circle: you need the definition's output (what medieval epistemology is) to generate its input (who the medieval epistemologists were). Without independent criteria for identifying medieval epistemologists, the definition cannot get off the ground.

=== STYLE brief 923164dc-b601-4f9d-b802-9df5832d093f
-- step 0 [4920155d-68bf-426a-ab2e-b6dba19d1759] label='Conclusion'
Medieval epistemology should be defined as the epistemological beliefs held by medieval epistemologists.
-- step 1 [cc94239a-a792-4692-9eee-2c8a5d344d84] label='Evidence'
Medieval epistemology is complex, and scholars have failed to produce a satisfactory definition. This proposed definition would include any belief held by any medieval epistemologist, even contradictory ones.
-- step 2 [5e40aace-a5bc-48ba-a76e-7b6acb2df46f] label='Evaluate'
The definition is potentially circular: to apply it, you must first identify who the medieval epistemologists were, which may require already knowing what medieval epistemology is.
-- step 3 [829f749f-ca5a-4c07-bbb2-41a5fa45f0cf] label='Goal'
Find the answer that most effectively exposes or exploits this definitional weakness.
-- note A [fa0b862b-37af-4c3e-8553-6bce5efc56e6]
Whether medieval and ancient epistemologists held the same beliefs is irrelevant. The definition's problem is about identifying who counts as a medieval epistemologist, not about the originality of their ideas.
-- note B [181d8d8b-e1a2-4d64-a8c1-6856efd46e75]
Medieval epistemologists being influenced by non-epistemological factors does not undermine the definition. You can still identify their epistemological beliefs regardless of what caused those beliefs.
-- note C [043d7715-d62c-4d6d-8312-59050b088460]
Their writings mixing epistemological and non-epistemological content just means sorting through material. That is a practical inconvenience, not a fundamental definitional failure.
-- note D [1b42ec66-1ddd-4585-9740-9e7e61bcc214]
Epistemologists contradicting each other just means the field contained diverse views. The definition explicitly accommodates contradictions — that is normal for any intellectual tradition.
-- note E [32f0c42d-d6c0-4f8e-8c01-ad8c0945c9c0]
If scholars cannot agree on who counts as a medieval epistemologist, the definition collapses. You need to identify the practitioners to apply it, but identifying practitioners requires already knowing what the field is — fatal circularity.

=== STYLE another_way 82503b1d-ea7d-476b-8994-1ddc67705956
-- step 0 [9e457663-8337-40bf-9af9-d5c8cca4ff21] label='Conclusion'
Someone thought they were being clever: "Medieval epistemology is whatever medieval epistemologists believed about epistemology." Sounds neat and tidy, right?
-- step 1 [32950dfa-59cb-4801-959f-b9b90b7846eb] label='Evidence'
The definition is motivated by frustration. Nobody can agree on what medieval epistemology IS, so this historian says: just look at what the practitioners believed. If they believed it, it counts. Contradictions welcome.
-- step 2 [5d704326-836e-434d-ab25-68a1e765bd2f] label='Evaluate'
But here is the problem: to use this definition, you first need to identify the medieval epistemologists. And how do you know who they are unless you already know what medieval epistemology IS? It is the philosophical equivalent of needing work experience to get your first job, but needing a job to get work experience. The definition eats its own tail.
-- step 3 [a2b15996-c8f8-445a-94fd-95608ba72369] label='Goal'
We need the answer that best exposes this circular trap — the one that shows why you cannot get the definition off the ground in the first place.
-- note A [2ed3faa8-ec62-48c2-8e4d-9388a58564a3]
"Medieval thinkers just recycled ancient Greek ideas!" Okay, but that does not break the definition. If someone defines "my Spotify playlist" as "songs I added to my playlist," the definition still works perfectly even if every song is a cover of a Beatles track. The content being unoriginal does not make the definition impossible to use. We need an answer that makes the definition collapse under its own weight, not one that makes the results unimpressive.
-- note B [bc082ab0-375a-4007-8253-27bd38c66e00]
"Their epistemology was influenced by religion and politics!" Name a philosopher whose was not. But more importantly, this does not make the definition stop working. If someone defines "Nicole's cooking" as "dishes Nicole makes," finding out Nicole was influenced by her Italian grandmother does not somehow make the definition unworkable. The causal backstory behind the beliefs is completely separate from whether we can identify and catalog them.
-- note C [34c5a802-8086-4f15-b41a-e7296cb6f03d]
"They wrote about other stuff too!" And? This is like saying a cookbook is hard to use because the author also included personal anecdotes between recipes. You just skip to the recipes. Yes, medieval philosophers wrote about theology, politics, and probably lunch. But you can still pull out their epistemological views. The definition's real problem is not that the source material is messy — it is something far more fundamental than a sorting exercise.
-- note D [34a203a4-66da-408d-b00f-ed798416d666]
Breaking news: people who think for a living sometimes think different things. If we disqualified every field whose practitioners disagreed, we would have to shut down philosophy, economics, medicine, and basically every department at every university. The definition ALREADY says contradictions are welcome — "if any believed the opposite, that is included too." This answer is pointing out a feature the historian explicitly designed into the system. It is like critiquing an umbrella for getting wet.
-- note E [0e1a1579-9dc9-485c-8529-ef1d22e77176]
This is the answer that makes the whole definition implode. Watch the logic eat itself:

"What is medieval epistemology?" "It is what medieval epistemologists believed!" "Great, who were the medieval epistemologists?" "The people who practiced medieval epistemology!" "...which is defined as...?" "What medieval epistemologists believed!"

And THIS answer twists the knife: scholars cannot even agree on who qualifies as a medieval epistemologist. So the definition is like a recipe that says "add the right amount of the correct ingredient" — completely useless unless you already have the answer. It is a philosophical Catch-22, and this answer drags it into the spotlight.

  A: The argument presumes, without providing justification, that if a person's promise is not selfishly motivated then that promise is reliable.
  B: The argument presumes, without providing justification, that promises made for selfish reasons are never kept.
  C: The argument confuses the effect of an action with its cause.
  D: The argument overlooks the fact that a promise need not be unreliable just because the person who made it had an ulterior motive for doing so.

BANK STIM: Early urban societies could not have been maintained without large-scale farming nearby.   A: Most peoples who lived in early times lived in areas near rivers or lakes.

  C: In early times it was not possible to maintain urban societies in areas far from rivers or lakes.
  D: Urban societies with farms near rivers or lakes do not have to rely upon irrigation to meet their farming needs.
  E: Early rural societies relied more on foraging than on agriculture for food.

BANK STIM: Cartographer: Maps are like language: they can be manipulated in order to mislead. That most people are not generally misled by words, however, should not lead us to think that most people are not susceptible to being misled by maps. Most people are taught to be cautious interpreters of language, but education in the sophisticated use of maps is almost nonexistent.
BANK STEM: Which one of the following most accurately describes how the statement that most people are taught to be cautious interpreters of language functions in the cartographer's argument?
  A: It is offered as an analogical case that helps to clarify the meaning of the argument's conclusion.
  B: It is a conclusion drawn from the claim that education in the sophisticated use of maps is almost nonexistent.
  C: It is part of a distinction drawn in order to support the argument's conclusion.
  D: It is offered as support for the contention that maps have certain relevant similarities to language.
  E: It is the conclusion drawn in the argument.
BANK KEY: C
TAGS: {'A': 'Contradiction', 'B': 'Wrong Role', 'C': 'Correct', 'D': 'Contradiction', 'E': 'Wrong Role'}

  A: In the city with the worst air pollution today, the air quality is better than it was 30 years ago.
  B: No city has worse air pollution today than it did 30 years ago.
  C: Most of the public outcries against pollution came from people in the cities that had the most polluted air.
  D: The most polluted cities today are not the cities that were the most polluted 30 years ago.

QID PT147-S4-Q3 | type Paradox | key D
BANK STIM: Hospital patients generally have lower infection rates and require shorter hospital stays if they are housed in private rooms rather than semiprivate rooms. Yet in Woodville’s hospital, which has only semiprivate rooms, infection rates and length of stays are typically the same as in several nearby hospitals where most of the rooms are private, even though patients served by these hospitals are very similar to those served by Woodville’s hospital.
BANK STEM: Which one of the following, if true, most helps to resolve the apparent conflict in the information above?
  A: Many of the doctors who routinely treat patients in Woodville’s hospital also routinely treat patients in one or more of the nearby hospitals.
  B: Most of the nearby hospitals were built within the last 10 years, whereas Woodville’s hospital was built about 50 years ago.
  C: Infection is more likely to be spread where people come into close contact with one another than where they do not.
  D: Woodville’s hospital has a policy of housing one patient per room in semiprivate rooms whenever possible.
  E: Woodville’s hospital is located in its central business district, whereas most of the nearby hospitals are located outside their municipalities’ business districts.
BANK KEY: D
TAGS: {'A': 'No Distinction / Opposite (if anything)', 'B': 'Opposite (if anything)', 'C': 'Restates Paradox / Unrelated to Goal', 'D': 'Correct', 'E': 'Unclear Impact / Thin Ice'}

=== STYLE single 46c441f2-55b0-43bd-8d9f-ff39165a1ad6
-- step 0 [d0121991-85a5-4f08-ba09-1400c6260735] label='Given that'
Patients in private rooms generally get fewer infections and shorter stays than patients in semiprivate rooms.
-- step 1 [97867ed9-4676-4509-a029-97c28772e0c9] label='How can it be that'
Woodville's hospital has only semiprivate rooms, yet its infection rates and stays match nearby hospitals with mostly private rooms and very similar patients?
-- step 2 [6ce9a2c6-21f1-4b5e-ae7b-5b4e9e47724f] label='Evaluate'
The patients are similar, so look at how the rooms are used. A semiprivate room is built for two, but if Woodville often puts only one patient in it, its patients get the benefit of a private room.
-- step 3 [a54f3f11-a758-43fc-b61b-6ea18276567d] label='Goal'
Find a difference between Woodville and the nearby hospitals that gives Woodville's patients the benefits of private rooms.
-- note A [8431f89a-1c8e-4cef-83bf-891a618b49f4]
We want a distinction between Woodville and nearby hospitals, and this provides a similarity.
-- note B [8d3a9954-93a4-4860-8ac7-f58b506d60b1]
We want a reason why Woodville is doing better than we'd expect, and being told that it is an older hospital seems more to be a reason why it would do worse than one might expect.
-- note C [811c847b-7416-4a0d-ad9d-2b8e8a5bb907]
This is reinforcing the background claim, explaining why private rooms tend to lead to lower infection rates and shorter hospital stays.
It does nothing to explain the surprise, which is why Woodville's semiprivate rooms seem to be doing as well as other hospitals' private rooms.
-- note D [c6089d54-79b2-469d-b09e-cc79015781f3]
We want a reason Woodville's semiprivate rooms do as well as other hospitals' private rooms, and this gives it.

If Woodville puts one patient in each semiprivate room whenever it can, then in practice most of its patients get a room to themselves. So Woodville gets the benefits of private rooms without having any, and the surprise goes away.
-- note E [b31dce01-5a21-4d11-95a9-6bacb7aa43f9]
For this to explain the surprise, we'd have to think that being located in the central business district has some common sense connection to lower infection rates or shorter hospital stays, which would definitely be walking on thin ice.

QID PT147-S4-Q4 | type Sufficient Assumption | key A
BANK STIM: Economist: Unemployment will soon decrease. If total government spending significantly increases next year, the economy will be stimulated in the short term and unemployment will decrease. If, on the other hand, total government spending significantly decreases next year, businesses will retain more of their earnings in the short term and employ more workers, thereby decreasing unemployment.
BANK STEM: The conclusion drawn by the economist is properly inferred if which one of the following is assumed?
  A: Either total government spending will significantly decrease next year or else total government spending will significantly increase next year.
  B: Government officials are currently implementing policies that are intended to reduce unemployment.
  C: If there is a significantly increased demand for workers, then there will be a significant decrease in unemployment.
  D: A significant increase in total government spending will slow the economy in the long run.
  E: If the economy is not stimulated and businesses do not retain more of their earnings, then unemployment will not decrease.
BANK KEY: A
TAGS: {'A': 'Correct', 'B': 'Unrelated to Goal: nothing about spending', 'C': 'Unrelated to Goal: nothing about spending', 'D': 'Unrelated to Goal: nothing about spending', 'E': 'Unrelated to Goal: nothing about spending'}

=== STYLE single 40ae62ac-de80-47c8-baec-cfabe66715ef
-- step 0 [b8630686-1d8d-4b91-bb33-e7c148dc0ee4] label='Conclusion'
Unemployment will soon decrease.
-- step 1 [1cef0c96-8d24-400f-8898-c1da4fa516e0] label='Evidence'
If total govt spending significantly goes up, unemployment will decrease.

If total govt spending significantly decreases, unemployment will decrease.
-- step 2 [e8c85123-28f1-4b94-801f-fcc6fe3a8a11] label='Evaluate'
We ascertain that the first claim is the conclusion by seeing that the other two claims are clearly meant to work together. Conjunctions/connections like "and / but / on the other hand" show us that two claims are interacting together to move the argument forward.

Each of the conditional premises involves TWO consequences, one of which is decreasing unemployment. I didn't bother writing out the other half of each outcome, because all we care about is proving that there will be decreasing unemployment.

At first, this argument seems pretty reasonable. It seems like an *Either Way You Slice It* style of argument: "Whether spending increases significantly or decreases significantly, we end up with decreasing unemployment. So either way you slice it, we're gonna have decreasing unemployment."

But we know that an argument on Sufficient Assumption can't *already* be airtight. There has to be some gap, so that the correct answer can make it airtight.

How would unemployment *fail to decrease*?

Well, I guess spending doesn't necessarily have to go significantly up or significantly down. It could more or less stay the same.

The author is presenting a *False Choice* (a false binary / a false dichotomy), acting like spending will either go up a bunch or down a bunch.

But she's failing to consider the middle ground, where spending basically stays the same.
-- step 3 [0cf755b5-c56e-473b-8db9-5095d3dd3436] label='Goal'
We just need to rule out the possibility that govt spending will stay basically the same. We need to know that it'll definitely go up or down significantly.
-- note A [b7a65957-2e87-44e7-a774-b4de9da7ce21]
The economist covers two cases: spending goes up significantly, or spending goes down significantly. Both lead to lower unemployment. But nothing says one of those two things will happen. Spending could stay about the same, and then neither rule applies.

This answer closes that gap. If spending will either rise or fall significantly, one of the two cases must happen, and either way unemployment decreases. The conclusion follows.
-- note B [e643f423-995d-406d-8018-f6de787bf37d]
Since this doesn't tell us whether spending will go up or down, it doesn't do the job we need it to do.
-- note C [38715b68-3d82-447a-9fb4-f83157a24af1]
Since this doesn't tell us whether spending will gone up or down, it doesn't do the job we need it to do.
-- note D [3edb70f9-f304-41d1-a775-ab6b64837d6c]
Since this doesn't tell us whether spending will go up or down, it doesn't do the job we need it to do.
-- note E [74abf194-80aa-4ac4-b219-d502bca5f8fc]
Since this doesn't tell us whether spending will go up or down, it doesn't do the job we need it to do.

QID PT156-S2-Q19 | type Weaken | key B
BANK STIM: Geologist: As a spinning object’s radius decreases, its rate
 of rotation increases; for example, figure skaters
 can twirl faster by bringing their arms closer to
 their sides. This law of physics helps to explain
 why, over the past decade, Earth’s rate of rotation
 increased. For earthquakes have increased in
 number and severity during the past decade; and
 since immediately after an earthquake Earth’s
 tectonic plates move toward the center of the
 earth, their movement causes Earth’s radius to
 decrease.
BANK STEM: Which one of the following, if true, would most
 undermine the geologist’s explanation of why Earth’s
 rate of rotation increased over the last decade?
  A: In the beginning of the twentieth century, geologists observed an increase in number and severity of earthquakes, accompanied by a decrease of Earth’s radius.
  B: During the past decade, other geological events have counteracted the movements of the tectonic plates that occur immediately after earthquakes.
  C: Only skillful figure skaters succeed in twirling faster by bringing their arms closer to their sides.
  D: Since the time of the ancient Egyptians, Earth’s rate of rotation has been known to fluctuate.
  E: Increased volcanic activity over the last ten years suggests that the overall movement of the tectonic plates has increased.
BANK KEY: B
TAGS: {'A': 'Opposite (if anything): more correlation strenthens, if anything', 'B': 'Correct', 'C': 'No Impact', 'D': 'No Impact', 'E': 'No Impact'}

=== STYLE single 4ce5c003-1fb6-429d-b62b-63eec4bf1563
-- step 0 [dfa97c6c-ed99-497b-9234-993f6337124a] label='Conclusion'
Earth's rotation sped up over the past decade because more earthquakes shrank Earth's radius.
-- step 1 [2b4a0393-35d7-4ad9-ab67-302885996589] label='Evidence'
A spinning object speeds up as its radius shrinks. Earthquakes increased in number and severity. Right after an earthquake, tectonic plates move toward Earth's center, shrinking the radius.
-- step 2 [5bed442a-0871-4c85-b185-6b44a3002a69] label='Evaluate'
The geologist assumes the plate movement after earthquakes produced a net decrease in the radius. If other forces pushed the other way, the radius may not have shrunk at all, and the explanation falls apart.
-- step 3 [67be5e38-0e7c-48b6-b0e6-d4997ffbd8df] label='Goal'
Show the radius may not have shrunk overall.
-- note A [35265abe-a620-4ba4-ac53-881bab2d7ae7]
Another case of more earthquakes going with a smaller radius supports the geologist, if anything.
-- note B [30f37111-d65c-4381-92c1-e5bdabace383]
Disrupts the assumption that the tectonic plate movement causes a net decrease in the Earth's radius by raising the possiblitiy that any decreases are counteracted by other movements.
-- note C [69fbcbba-ec6a-4a2c-9c49-13936db671d9]
Limiting the example to a subset of skaters doesn't change the law of physics the example is exemplifying.
-- note D [4af466bd-7891-4bec-ac69-7d794b98a2f6]
This might seem at first like an alternative explanation for the current rate of rotation increase. It isn't, however, because the reason for the historic fluctuations aren't specified. We can't, therefore, say the current change in rotation rate is any less likely to be due to the contraction of Earth's radius that it was before we knew that similar rotation fluctuations have happened throughout history.
-- note E [4ed90aec-1a55-4bc7-b926-12de0fb40ada]
The overall amount of tectonic movement increasing in the last decade doesn't attack the idea that the movement ultimately leads to a net decrease in the size of the Earth.
